Committee for Medicinal Products for Human Use (CHMP)

## Assessment report

## Etcamah

International non-proprietary name: camizestrant

Procedure No. EMEA/H/C/006494/0000

## Note

Assessment report as adopted by the CHMP with all information of a commercially confidential nature deleted. Additional information has been redacted in order to protect data integrity for the ongoing blinded pivotal trial SERENA-6; this information will be made publicly available once the SERENA-6 full results have been submitted to EMA.

The Guardant360 CDx test provides tumour mutation profiling through detection of single nucleotide variants (SNVs), insertions and deletions (indels) in seventy-four (74) genes, copy number amplifications (NAs) in eighteen (18) genes, fusions in six (6) genes, and microsatellite instability (MSI)-High status. The test is intended to be used by qualified health care professionals as a companion diagnostic to identify patients who may benefit from treatment with the therapies approved accordingly. Patients who are negative for the biomarkers listed should be reflexed to tissue biopsy testing using a tumour tissue test, if feasible. EMA and national scientific advice have been received on the camizestrant quality, pre-clinical and clinical development to support marketing authorisation in the EU.

The EMA SA from September 2024 included the following key CHMP recommendations:

- The CHMP was of the opinion that an early PFS benefit at an interim analysis (IA) (i.e., 135 events as per protocol, information fraction 69%, 43% maturity), is unlikely to be considered sufficient to declare superiority from a clinical perspective as the need for support from PFS2 and OS data will be considered key.
- The CHMP indicated that support by PFS2 of good quality will be important for contextualisation of PFS effect within the patient's treatment strategy.
- The CHMP raised concerns on the interchangeability between the different CDK4/6 inhibitors in the proposed indication. The study design was revised to include all 3 CDK4/6 inhibitors (palbociclib, ribociclib, and abemaciclib), and the randomisation was stratified per CDK4/6 inhibitor.
- The CHMP recommended to justify the choice of 6 months on AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment before screening patients at time of submission.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- The CHMP was of the opinion that ESR1m surveillance of 8 to 12 weeks could introduce additional variability and ideally should be more tightly defined to e.g., every 8 (± 1) weeks within a prospective trial setting.
- The CHMP acknowledged that the effect of early change of ET cannot be disentangled from the effect associated with continuation with CDK4/6 inhibitor and no data are available yet comparing camizestrant with fulvestrant.
- In addition, specific CHMP scientific advice was sought on the proposed overall clinical development plan (PK, efficacy and safety) to support the benefit-risk assessment for a CDK4/6 inhibitor agnostic indication (EMA/SA/0000180143).

Confirmation of European Union Paediatric Investigation Plan class waiver applicability for camizestrant has been received from EMA as adopted by the Paediatric committee during the plenary meeting held from 21 to 24 June 2022 (EMEA-01-2022). The Paediatric committee confirmed that camizestrant proposed for the 'treatment of hormone receptor positive breast cancer' falls under the scope of Agency Decision CW/0001/2015.

## 1.3. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant AstraZeneca AB submitted on 29 May 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Etcamah (camizestrant), through the centralised procedure falling within the Article 3(1) and point 3 of Annex of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 25 January 2024.

The applicant applied for the following indication:

Etcamah (camizestrant) in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR)-positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2)-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation during first-line endocrine-based therapy.

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, Etcamah (camizestrant) plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist, in accordance with current clinical practice standards.

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, and nonclinical and clinical data based on applicant's own tests and studies.

Based on the review of available data on the active substance, the CHMP considers that camizestrant is to be qualified as a new active substance in itself, as it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.11.3. Opinion

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy, the CHMP considers by majority decision that the benefit-risk balance of ETCAMAH is favourable in the following indication(s):

Etcamah in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with ER-positive, HER2-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation and without disease progression during first-line endocrine therapy in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (for biomarker based patient-selection, see section 4.2 and 5.1).

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, Etcamah plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist or antagonist.

The CHMP, therefore, recommends the granting of the marketing authorisation subject to the conditions described in the following sections.

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

## Disease

Breast cancer ranks as the second most prevalent cancer globally, accounting for 11.6% of all cancer cases (Bray et al 2024). It is the most commonly diagnosed cancer in women, comprising 23.8% of all cancer diagnoses. In 2022, over 2.3 million new cases of breast cancer were identified worldwide (Bray et al 2024). Although many patients are cured of their early-stage disease due to advances in local/regional and systemic treatment, advanced breast cancer (ABC) remains incurable. Approximately 70% of patients present with HR-positive (ER-positive and/or PgR positive), HER2negative disease (American Cancer Society 2024-2025), making it the most common subtype in both women and men. Over two-thirds of breast cancers express the oestrogen receptor (ER), which is a key driver of breast cancer initiation and progression (Bray et al. 2024). Various studies have reported that the frequency of ESR1m is low (approximately 4%) at the initial diagnosis of HR-positive, HER2 negative ABC (Bardia et al 2020, Goetz et al 2024, Hortobagyi et al 2018). However, approximately 40% of patients at the end of first-line (1L) treatment of aromatase inhibitor (AI) + cyclin-dependent kinase 4/6 (CDK4/6) inhibitor have detectable ESR1m in ctDNA, and the prevalence of ESR1m rises further to &gt; 50% in patients who have been treated with AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor for more than one year (Bhave et al 2024, Bidard et al 2022, Cardoso et al 2023, Chaudhary et al 2024).

## Current therapies and unmet medical need

The preferred standard 1L treatment for patients with locally advanced/metastatic HR positive, HER2 negative breast cancer is the combination of CDK4/6 inhibitors with endocrine therapy (ET) (fulvestrant or AI) based on the results of several Phase III trials and current clinical guidelines. However, most patients eventually experience disease progression on this regimen.

A common resistance mechanism to current ET is the emergence of ESR1m, the gene encoding ERα (Rinaldi et al 2020). Evidence suggests that detection of ESR1m is associated with poor treatment outcomes in terms of PFS and OS (Turner et al 2020, Turner et al 2023), mainly owing to the lack of effective treatment options to address this driver mutation.

There are currently no 1L therapies specifically approved for the treatment of patients with HRpositive, HER2 negative ABC with emergent ESR1m, ahead of disease progression. Progression beyond 1L is often associated with increased toxicities of therapy regimens and diminished treatment efficacy, likely due to the increased genetic complexity of disease and increased tumour burden. Currently, Orserdu (elacestrant ) and Inluriyo (imlunestrant) are approved monotherapy options for patients with ESR1m who have progressed on ET + CDK4/6 inhibitor, and PFS increase has been shown ((median PFS 3.8 months with elacestrant vs 1.9 months for ET's (including AI and fulvestrant) and mPFS 5.5 months with imlunestrant vs 3.8 months for ET's (including AI and fulvestrant), (Orserdu EPAR, Inluriyo EPAR)).

## 2.3. Description of the product

Camizestrant is an oral selective SERD and estrogen receptor (ER) antagonist. Camizestrant binds to the ligand binding domain of ERα, antagonising the activity of ERα encoded by both wild-type ESR1 and ESR1m, and inducing proteasome-dependent degradation of ERα, without agonising ERα.

The therapeutic indication applied for was:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Etcamah in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR)-positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2)-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation during first-line endocrine-based therapy.

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, Etcamah plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist, in accordance with current clinical practice standards.

The final approved therapeutic indication is:

Etcamah in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with ER-positive, HER2-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation and without disease progression during first-line endocrine therapy in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (for biomarker based patient-selection, see section 4.2 and 5.1).

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, Etcamah plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist or antagonist.

The recommended dose of Etcamah in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor is 75 mg once daily. The CDK4/6 inhibitor should be continued at the same dose at the time of initiation with Etcamah as when the ESR1m was detected. For the recommended dosing information reference is advised to the SmPC of the CDK4/6 inhibitor or LHRH agonist or antagonist. If the CDK4/6 inhibitor is permanently discontinued, Etcamah should also be discontinued.

Camizestrant is not currently approved for marketing in any country.

## 2.4. Inspection issues

## 2.4.1. Good manufacturing practice (GMP) inspection(s)

Proof of GMP compliance for all manufacturing and testing sites is available. No inspection was deemed required.

## 2.4.2. Good laboratory practice (GLP) inspection(s)

All pivotal non-clinical safety pharmacology and toxicology studies were conducted in Organisation of Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) member countries under an extensive GLP audit program with the majority of studies being conducted at European located Laboratories. In general, the studies appeared to be GLP compliant.

No inspection was considered to be additionally required.

## 2.4.3. Good clinical practice (GCP) inspection(s)

No inspection was considered to be additionally required. GCP inspection information from the US FDA was also considered during this assessment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as film-coated tablets containing 75 mg of camizestrant as active substance.

Other ingredients are:

Tablet core: microcrystalline cellulose (type 102), calcium hydrogen phosphate, sodium starch glycolate (Type A) and magnesium stearate

Tablet coating: polyvinyl alcohol, titanium dioxide (E171), macrogol 3350, talc, iron oxide red (E172), iron oxide yellow (E172) and iron oxide black (E172).

The product is available in aluminium/aluminium perforated unit dose blisters, as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The applicant provided full details of the manufacturing and control of the active substance.

The chemical name of camizestrant is N-[1-(3-Fluoropropyl)-3-azetidinyl]-6-[(6S,8R)- 8-methyl-7(2,2,2-trifluoroethyl)-6,7,8,9- tetrahydro-3H-pyrazolo[4,3-f]isoquinolin-6-yl]-3-pyridinamine corresponding to the molecular formula C24H28F4N6. It has a relative molecular mass of 476.51 and the following structure, scheme 1:

Scheme 1. Camizestrant chemical structure

<!-- image -->

The chemical structure of the active substance was elucidated by a combination of mass spectrometry (MS), 1 H and 13 C nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopy, infrared spectroscopy (IR), elemental analysis and ultraviolet spectroscopy.

The absolute configuration and crystal status of camizestrant have been confirmed by single crystal Xray diffraction (SXRD).

Camizestrant is a crystalline powder that is slightly soluble in water (0.14 mg/mL) and very soluble (&gt; 1.2 mg/mL) below pH 4.5.

Camizestrant exhibits stereoisomerism due to the presence of two chiral centres, which are in the 6S,

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

8R configuration Chiral purity is controlled routinely by chiral Supercritical Fluid Chromatography (SFC) which is specific for all the four possible stereoisomers

Extensive polymorph screening has confirmed the existence of solvated and non-solvated crystal modifications of camizestrant. The manufacturing process from the applicant consistently produces camizestrant of a non-solvated crystalline form. Stability studies on camizestrant showed that there is no conversion to other forms under long-term, accelerated or stressed conditions (see stability section).

Camizestrant is a non-hygroscopic stable molecule and is only slowly degraded in basic, acidic or oxidative conditions.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation, and process controls

Description of manufacturing process and process controls

Satisfactory GMP documentation for drug substance manufacturing sites has been provided.

The synthesis of camizestrant can be described as a 3-step convergent synthesis starting from well defined starting materials.

Proposed starting materials are acceptable

Starting materials and isolated intermediates, critical parameters are defined by the applicant.

A design space is not claimed. Proven acceptable ranges (PARs) have been defined for the manufacturing process from factorial experimental design (FED) or one variable at a time (OVAT) studies.

Manufacturing process development

Throughout development the conversion of the starting material to camizestrant has used the same bond-forming steps. Several minor adjustments have been incorporated over time. These have been adequately described. Briefly, the evolution of the synthetic process can be divided into five distinct 'generations'. Generation 1 and 2 are classed as early-phase development version of the process (generation 1 was used to manufacture pre-clinical batches of camizestrant). Generation 3 is classed as mid-development version of the process. Generations 4 and 5 are classed as late-development versions of the process. Generation 5 is the commercial manufacturing process. The other generations have provided some of the material used for batches for toxicology studies, etc.

## 3.2.3. Specification

## Specifications

The active substance specification includes tests for description, identification (FT-IR), assay (LC), organic impurities (LC), polymorphic form (XRD), residual solvents (GC), particle size distribution (laser diffraction) and sulphated ash (Ph.Eur.).

The EU/ICH Q3A thresholds are applied (maximum daily dose is not defined. Daily dose is 75 mg):

Reporting level: 0.05%; ID threshold: 0.10%; Qualification threshold: 0.15 %.

During the review the applicant was requested to tighten the assay limit and several impurity limits in line with batch data from commercial generation 5 synthesis and not use historical batch data and to include a test for residue of ignition or sulphated ash. This was satisfactorily addressed.

Acceptable justifications for the omission of water content, stereoisomeric impurities, palladium, elemental impurities, other residual solvents, microbiological quality and mutagenic impurities have been provided.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A discussion of the present and potential impurities, including mutagenic impurities has been

presented. The information is considered satisfactory. Impurities present at higher than the qualification threshold according to ICH Q3A were qualified by toxicological and clinical studies and appropriate specifications have been set. Analytical procedures and reference standards The analytical methods for assay (LC), organic impurities (LC) and Residual solvents (GC) have been validated. Polymorphic form (by XRPD) has been validated for specificity and PSD (by laser diffraction) has been validated for Precision, accuracy and robustness. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards has been presented. Batch analysis Batch analysis data from eleven commercial scale batches of the active substance manufactured using the proposed manufacturing process (Generation 5) are provided. The results are within the specifications and consistent from batch to batch. Container closure The active substance is stored in low-density polyethylene (LDPE) bags, which are placed in a sealed aluminium foil bag. The aluminium bag is placed within a rigid outer container. The primary packaging material complies with regulation EU No. 10/2011 (and its amendments).

Specification and a typical IR spectrum for LDPE is provided.

## 3.2.4. Stability

Stability data from three pilot scale batches of camizestrant from the mid development process (generation 3, from a development site) and supportive stability studies of three commercial scale batches of camizestrant from the late development process proposed for commercial use (generation 5) from the proposed commercial manufacturing site stored in the intended commercial package were presented. Changes between generation 3 and generation 5 were minor; they were intended to improve process robustness and efficiency. There were no significant changes in the final stages to form AZD9833 crude and camizestrant. The quality of AZD9833 crude and camizestrant batches manufactured during the mid development and the late development processes are comparable. As a result, the primary stability batches are deemed representative of the batches produced by the commercial process.

Samples were stored for up to 24 months under long term conditions (25 ºC / 60% RH and intermediate conditions 30°C/75% RH), and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines. Photostability testing following the ICH guideline Q1B was performed on one batch.

The following parameters were tested: description, assay, organic impurities, stereoisomeric impurities, polymorphic form, particle size distribution, water content, microbial quality and water activity. The analytical methods used were the same as for release and were stability indicating

No significant changes were observed in any of the attributes tested under any of the storage conditions. content. The data confirms that camizestrant is stable when exposed to light.

With regards to the ongoing stability program any confirmed out-of-specification result, or significant negative trend, should be reported to the Rapporteur and EMA.

Results on stress conditions were also provided. Thermal stability of camizestrant in solid state was assessed for camizestrant at 50°C for 3 months, hydrolytic stability was assessed by storing camizestrant at 40°C/75% RH for 6 months. No significant degradation was observed under any of the conditions studied, demonstrating that camizestrant is inherently stable in the solid state In addition, forced degradation studies were performed to elucidate degradation pathways and to confirm the suitability of the analytical methods applied to the stability studies using camizestrant solutions in pH 1 (acidic), pH 7 (neutral), pH 13 (basic) under high temperature, oxidative conditions (hydrogen peroxide, 4,4'-azobis(4-cyanovaleric acid) (ACVA, copper (II) chlorideand iron (III) chloride Degradation of camizestrant was observed under strong acidic, basic and oxidative (hydrogen peroxide, ACVA, Cu(II)Cl2 and Fe(III)Cl3) when in solution.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed supplier is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 36 months when stored at or below 30°C in the proposed container.

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

The finished product is an immediate release film-coated tablet for oral administration in the strength of 75 mg packed in blister packaging.

The finished product is a beige, round, biconvex, film-coated tablet, approximately 9 mm in diameter and debossed with 'CM' above '75' on one side and plain on the reverse.5

## Pharmaceutical development

The aim of the pharmaceutical development was to develop a once daily immediate release oral dosage form of camizestrant. The early development program of camizestrant covered three tablet strengths, 25, 75 and 100 mg. The 75 mg strength was selected for commercialization.

As indicated in the active substance section, camizestrant is a non-hygroscopic highly soluble nonsolvated crystalline solid. Its polymorphic form and particle size are controlled in the active substance specification.

Polymorphic form within the finished product was monitored throughout development confirming the presence of the proposed commercial polymorphic form of camizestrant.

The chosen excipients are conventional in pharmaceuticals and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation. Given the continuous manufacturing (CM) process the applicant was requested to discuss the functionality related characteristics (FRC) of the excipients and update their specifications accordingly. The limits were sufficiently justified based on the excipient batches used to manufacture the DoE batches and process validation batches.

The formulation development has sufficiently shown that the chosen formulation is suitable for filmcoated tablet that fufill the quality target product profile for an immediate release tablet.

Process robustness to excipient variability include attributes that is potentially relevant to continuous direct compression (CDC) process robustness.

The formulation has been demonstrated to be robust towards minor changes in excipients amounts and coating weight gain.

In the original submission, the applicant proposed an in-silico model for disintegration (based on porosity of the core tablets and coating weight gain) as a surrogate for disintegration to support real time release testing. The justification was based on ICH Q6A and the criteria outlined in its decision tree 7(1). Disintegration testing in lieu of dissolution testing was not considered acceptable by CHMP as the dissolution method development was not considered sufficient and it could not be concluded that the dissolution is rapid as per ICH Q6A or that disintegration testing is more discriminatory than dissolution (MO 1).To address this MO the applicant presented a more thorough discussion and further clarifications on the dissolution method development, and included dissolution testing in the specification in addition to disintegration testing. The early development work of camizestrant drug product included a formulation selection study focused on screening of chemical stability and manufacturability of various tablet prototypes. Prototype tablet formulations containing different diluents, disintegrants and lubricant were produced through a roller compaction (RC) process. The diluents were selected to obtain a combination of plastic and brittle compression properties of the prototype formulations and to provide robust mechanical properties for a dry manufacturing process. A specific drug load was chosen to enable production of relevant tablet strengths for the clinical program by using a common powder blend or granule. By comparing the results from chemical stability, processability and dissolution performance, the most suitable prototype composition to proceed with for the early clinical program was selected. This formulation was deemed suitable for continued clinical supply. Further development work was conducted during phase 3 to change the manufacturing process from RC to CDC process for commercial use. Camizestrant film-coated tablets produced through RC and CDC are of the same qualitative and quantitative composition. Bridging between the RC and DC tablets (representative of the CDC tablets) was justified by comparative in vitro dissolution pH 1.2, 4.5 and 6.8 and comparative plasma exposures generated in a relative bioavailability study in conjunction with dosing of an oral solution (study D8530C00004). In vitro studies showed that dissolution was very rapid (85% or more dissolved within 15 minutes) in all media. The bioavailability study showed similarity in exposure between the RC and DC tablets and between RC and DC tablets and the oral solution. These data demonstrate there is no impact of manufacturing process technology on camizestrant plasma exposure and therefore the change to CDC during pivotal clinical studies has no impact on finished product in vivo performance. The development of the CDC process started with a material characterisation study and proof of concept study to demonstrate the suitability of CDC as manufacturing technology for camizestrant tablets but also to identify suitable target parameter settings for production of clinical trial material. Loss in weight feeders are used to add the active and excipients into a blender for mixing. After blending the blend is compressed into tablet cores. Up to and including tablet compression the process is in continuous manufacturing mode and coating of the tablet cores is performed in batch mode. The applicant has not applied QbD principles in the development of the finished product and their manufacturing process. A factorial DoE experiment is performed that included 4 factors. Detailed results of these experiments are not provided and a short discussion and conclusion by the applicant is included. The control strategy described in the original submission was insufficiently documented and critical information was missing from dossier sections P.2.3., P.3.3 and P.3.4. e.g, justification of the proposed process parameters ranges, description of the mechanism is in place to evaluate the consistency of the process (e.g. alert and action limits), residence time distribution (RTD) characteristics, information about the loss in weight feeders and the in-line blender, diversion and rejection mechanisms, characterisation of the process dynamics, description of the sampling strategy for the NIR method and equipment design and system integration. This led to MO2 and MO3. In addition, the control strategy in the original submission also included real time release testing (RTRT) for identification of the active substance, assay, uniformity of dosage units and disintegration. RTRT had not been adequately supported by data in line with the CHMP guideline on RTRT (EMA/CHMP/QWP/811210/2009-Rev1), section 6.2 and a valid proof of GMP compliance including RTRT for the manufacturing site was missing (MO6). The applicant acknowledged the concerns and updated the dossier providing further context to the control strategy. The applicant also reported issues with the NIR equipment and NIR model prior to Process Performance Qualification (PPQ) and the start of commercial manufacturing, and given the failed attempts to re-establish the NIR model to support process validation activities decided to withdraw the NIR methods from the proposed control strategy. Consequently, at line liquid chromatography (LC) stratified testing to control the process was established.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The control strategy for the continuous manufacturing process includes active process controls and in process testing. The strategy for start-up, pause and shutdown mitigations to prevent material buildup and assessment of process robustness to variability of active substance and excipient characteristics has been defined.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

## Manufacture

Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided for all sites involved in the manufacturing, testing and batch release of the finished product.

The proposed batch size is defined by the input material.

The manufacturing process consists of four main steps: feeding/milling/mixing, compression, filmcoating and packaging.

2The manufacturing process is non-standard as it includes continuous manufacturing. , The core tablets are obtained by continuous manufacturing and the film-coated is performed in a batch-mode.

As requested by CHMP during the review the description of the manufacturing process was revised to include process parameters with defined and acceptable proven ranges, a summary of start-up, shutdown, pause and restart procedures, the equipment design and the sampling plan and detailed diversion strategy.

Proven acceptable ranges (PARs) have been defined for several steps of the manufacturing process. The available development data, the proposed control strategy and batch analysis data from commercial scale batches fully support the proposed PARs.

A holding time for the uncoated core tablets of up to 90 days at below 25°C has been proposed.

To support this hold time, two commercial scale batches of core camizestrant tablets, 75 mg, manufactured at the commercial site packed in double LDPE bags inside an aluminium foil bag with desiccants were placed on stability at long-term condition (25°C/60% RH) for 90 days. Samples were tested for the same parameters as in the finished product stability studies (see section below). No changes were observed in any of the attributes tested and therefore the proposed holding time is acceptable.

## Process controls

The in-process controls are sufficiently described, as well as the active process controls. The sampling plan for the IPCs is described.

## Process validation / verification

Satisfactory process validation data has been provided by the applicant as requested by CHMP in a scientific advice and during the MAA review (MO 4). Manufacturing process validation is shown regarding start-up, pauses, shutdown and the state control during manufacturing of validation batches.It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner. The in-process controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.3. Product specification

Specifications

:

The finished product release and shelf life specifications include appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form: description (visual), identification (LC/UV), assay (LC), degradation products (LC), disintegration (Ph.Eur.), dissolution (in-house), uniformity of dosage units by weight variation (Ph.Eur.) and microbiological quality (TAMC, TYMC, E.coli) (Ph.Eur.).

T6

In the original submission the applicant proposed a NIR spectroscopy method (test done just before tablet compression) as the primary method for identification, assay and UDU. However, since critical documentation for real time release testing (RTRT) in line with the CHMP guideline on RTRT was missing (e.g. relationship between end-product testing, material attributes, process monitoring and acceptance criteria, suitable scoped MIA/GMP certificate for the site in question, minimum acceptable sample size for the RTRT, substantial comparative data at commercial scale -parallel testing-) CHMP raised a MO (MO 6). Due to performance issues with the NIR equipment/model, these were withdrawn by the applicant during the review.

In addition, as discussed above, disintegration as a surrogate for dissolution was originally proposed but was not considered sufficiently justified by CHMP (MO1). The applicant was requested to develop a suitable dissolution method and include dissolution in the finished product specification.

Description and identification acceptance criteria are acceptable. Assay limit at release and shelf-life (tightened during the review) is also acceptable . Disintegration limits at release and at shelf-life are sufficiently justified. The degradation product limits were tightened during the review and are considered acceptable. Uniformity of dosage units and microbiological acceptance criteria is acceptable.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product has been assessed following a risk-based approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Batch analysis data on three commercially representative batches using a validated ICP-MS method was provided, demonstrating that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE. Based on the risk assessment and the presented batch data it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls. The information on the control of elemental impurities is satisfactory.

Following the request from CHMP (MO5), a risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product was provided considering all suspected and actual root causes in line with the \"Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/409815/2020) and the \"Assessment report- Procedure under Article 5(3) of Regulation EC (No) 726/2004- Nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/369136/2020). Since camizestrant is intended for the treatment of cancer patients with serious and life-threatening malignancies as defined by guidance ICH S9 'Non-clinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceuticals'. N-Nitrosamine impurities controlled according to ICH Q3B guidelines. The risk assessment considered the active substance, excipients, manufacturing process, packaging materials and stability of the tablets. In the active substance, two potential nitrosamines have been identified in addition to the nitrosamine of camizestrant, although it has been concluded there is no risk for formation of these above the permitted level of 0.10% in the active substance. Camizestrant is a secondary amine, and an evaluation of the formulation composition and manufacturing process identified the potential presence of nitrites in the excipients at levels &lt;10 ppm. A calculation has been performed to assess the theoretical amount of nitrosamines that could potentially form assuming the reaction goes to 100% completion using the daily dose of 75 mg. This calculated amount is significantly below (&lt;10%) the ICH Q3B identification limits of 0.2%. Based on the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Sufficient justification has also been provided for not including residual solvents, stereoisomeric impurities, water content and physical form in the finished product specification.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines.

Stress studies of the finished product show that the analytical methods are stability indicating.

The only reference standard used for routine analysis of the finished product is the camizestrant reference standard and a reference is given to active substance module, where it is sufficiently detailed.

## Batch analysis

Batch analysis data has been presented for multiple batches including the initial clinical batches and three commercial scale batches manufactured by CDC used for process validation. The batch analysis shows similar results confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification. Container closure

The finished product is packed in aluminium/aluminium perforated unit dose blistes The material in direct contact with the drug product is confirmed to comply with EU 10/2011. The choice of the container closure system has been validated by stability data and is adequate for the intended use of the product.

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

Stability data from three pilot and one commercial scale batches of finished product stored for up to 36 months at long-term (25 ºC / 60% RH) and intermediate (30°C/75% RH) storage conditions and 6 months at accelerated storage conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to ICH guidelines were provided.

The batches were manufactured using commercial process, except for the debossing, which is not expected to impact any conclusions. The batches were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing.

Samples were tested for description, assay, degradation products, disintegration, dissolution, water content and microbiological quality.

Stability data showed no significant change in description, assay, degradation products, dissolution, water content or microbiological quality under any of the conditions tested. A slight increase in disintegration time was observed. This trend was consistent across all batches in the primary stability studies. However, the change is not considered to have any impact on the quality or performance of the product.

In addition, one batch was exposed to light as defined in the ICH Guideline on Photostability Testing of New Drug Substances and Products. The data confirm that the finished product is not photolabile.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 36 months with no storage conditions as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) are acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In order to support bulk hold times between the end of manufacture and primary packaging, two pilto scale batches of camizestrant film-coated tablets manufactured at the commercial site were packed in aluminium foil bags and set down on stability in accordance with ICH Q1A at long-term condition (25 °C/60% RH) for up to 24 months and accelerated condition (40°C/75% RH) for 6 months . No change in description, assay, degradation products, dissolution, water content or microbiological quality was observed A slight increase in disintegration time was observed. However, the changes were not significant and have demonstrated no impact on the quality or performance of the product. Therefore, the proposed bulk holding time of the film-coated tablets of 24 months is acceptable.

## 3.3.5. Post approval change management protocol(

Not applicable.

## 3.3.6. Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner. The finished product is manufactured using a hybrid CM mode (i.e. some unit operations operate in a batch mode while others are integrated and operate in a continuous mode). Several MOs were raised during the procedure concerning: MO1 on the development of the dissolution method and the proposal for and in-silico model for disintegration as a surrogate for dissolution to support real time release testing; MO2 and MO3 on the lack of relevant information in P.2.3. to support the proposed control strategy and the insufficiently documented control strategy; MO4 on the missing process validation results on the finished product; MO5 on the incomplete risk assessment for potential nitrosamine impurities; and and MO6. on the original proposal to use RTRT as part of the control strategy. Acceptable documentation was provided to address each MO. The NIR method and RTRT proposal was withdrawn. The proposed control strategy consists of material attributes of the input materials, set-points and/or PARs for process parameters, process monitoring and control, the strategy for diversion and collection of non-conforming material, the equipment design, system integration and end product testing. The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

## 3.5. Recommendations for future quality development

Not applicable.

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

Camizestrant is a next generation oral selective estrogen receptor degrader (ngSERD) and complete estrogen receptor (ER) antagonist. Camizestrant has been tested in a range of ER-positive breast cancer PDX models that harbour either wild type or mutant ESR1 and has been tested in combination with a CDK4/6i. ADME studies to describe the pharmacokinetic profile in rats and dogs were performed. Camizestrant has also been tested in a panel of safety pharmacology GLP studies, and secondary pharmacology and cardiac ion channel screens. Camizestrant has been evaluated in repeat dose oral toxicology GLP studies of up to six months duration in rat, and up to 9-months duration in dog. In addition, in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies, reproductive toxicology studies (assessing male fertility on the 6-month rat toxicity study, embryofoetal development with pre- and post-natal assessments in rats and female fertility and early embryonic development in rats) and an in vitro phototoxicity study have been conducted according to GLP. Mechanistic studies related to heart rate reduction and visual effects have been conducted. A 1-month rat GLP study has been conducted for the purpose of impurity qualification. Scientific advice was obtained EMA/CHMP/SAWP/528818/ 2020, Procedure No.: EMEA/H/SA/4613/1/2020/III regarding CHMP recommendations on cardiovascular safety, which have been adequately addressed in the MAA for camizestrant with the exception of mechanistic studies to explore potential mechanisms of non-hERG-related QT prolongation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Camizestrant was quantified in rat, dog and monkey serum using extraction via protein precipitation and then analysed by liquid chromatography with mass spectrometric detection (LC-MS/MS). Samples from the GLP rat (28-day and 6-month), GLP dog toxicology studies (28-day and 9-month), safety pharmacology studies and investigative dog cardiovascular studies were extracted via protein precipitation and subsequently analysed for camizestrant using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Bioanalytical methods developed for quantitation of serum camizestrant in rat and in dog GLP toxicity and safety pharmacology studies were considered sufficiently validated and fit for purpose.

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1.1. In vitro pharmacology

Several in vitro studies were conducted in support of the suggested mode of action (MoA) of camizestrant (AZD9833) to evaluate its estrogen receptor binding affinity, specificity and efficacy.

## Estrogen receptor (ER) binding:

In an in vitro competitive binding assay using human wild-type (WT) ERα and ERβ ligand-binding domains (LBDs) and a fluorescent marked ER ligand (fluormone ES2), camizestrant displaced the ER ligand with IC50 values of 2.4 nM and 11.3 nM for WT ERα and ERβ, respectively. Additionally, camizestrant also displaced the ER ligand from ERα LBDs containing clinically relevant ESR1 mutations (Y537N, Y537C, S463P, D538G, Y537S and E380Q) with IC50 values of 2.0, 2.2, 2.9, 6.7, 13.6 and 13.7 nM, respectively. Selectivity was shown by a &gt;4000-fold difference in IC50 values to closely related nuclear hormone receptors, androgen receptor (AR) and progesterone receptor (PR) with an IC50 of &gt; 100000 nM and 59790 nM, respectively.

## ERα degradation:

Camizestrant downregulated ERα in a dose-dependent manner with an IC50 of 0.2 nM in an in vitro plate-based immunofluorescence assay in ER+ MCF7 breast cancer cells. Reduction in ERα protein levels (100%) were shown by Western Blot to be comparable between camizestrant and fulvestrant at saturated concentrations of 100 nM in a panel of nine ER-positive cell lines (MCF7, CAMA-1, T47D, BT474, MDA-MB-134, MDA-MB-361, ZR-75-1, HCC1428 and an ER+ve human endometrial cell line

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(Ishikawa)). No effect on ERβ or androgen receptor (AR) was noted in MCF7 cells under the same condition, indicating that camizestrant is selective for ERα. Addition of a proteasome inhibitor (MG132) counteracted ERα reduction in MCF7 cells, indicating that the observed decrease in ERα protein was due to proteasome-dependent degradation mechanisms. Camizestrant dependent degradation was further confirmed in a SILAC assay, where heavy-labelled ERα peptide was tracked over time to measure ERα half-life in two independent experiments, and half-life was reduced from 2.54 and 3.86 hours in DMSO to 0.70 and 0.46 hours in the presence of camizestrant. Antagonistic effect of camizestrant: The ability of camizestrant to antagonize ERα activity was evaluated by reduction in progesterone receptor (PR) protein expression levels by using immunofluorescence in wild-type MCF7 cells in the presences of estradiol, or in engineered MCF7(Y537S) cells with a Y537S ESR1 mutation. Camizestrant antagonized PR expression with an IC50 concentration of 0.3 nM in MCF7 cells and 7.7 nM in MCF7(Y537S) cells. Maximal PR downregulation was equivalent to fulvestrant at 100nM. RNA sequencing, presented as a heatmap, was used to determine the effect of camizestrant on a core set of common oestradiol-induced and oestradiol-repressed genes in MCF7 and CAMA1 cells. Camizestrant (100nM) downregulated the expression of all ER-regulated genes normally increased by oestradiol, such as PGR, TFF1 and GREB1, and likewise, camizestrant increased the expression of all genes whose expression was normally decreased by oestradiol. Comparable results were observed at whole transcriptome level, where camizestrant (100 nM) completely reversed all oestradiol-induced gene expression changes in three ER+ cell lines (MCF7, CAMA1 and BT474c) without modulating any genes that were not modulated by oestradiol. These data confirmed camizestrant as a selective ER antagonist. ER antagonistic activity and selectivity was confirmed in a panel of cell lines expressing related nuclear hormone receptors (NHRs) sharing structural homology in core elements, including the LBD. Camizestrant showed a high level of antagonism against human ERα and ERβ with 88 and 95% inhibition, respectively, and minimal activity (&lt; 25% inhibition) against other receptors in the panel. Lack of agonistic effect of camizestrant: In order to show that camizestrant does not act as a full or partial agonist (like tamoxifen), levels of PR expression, a marker of transcriptional ER activity, were assessed by immunofluorescence in two ER-positive cell lines (MCF7 and Ishikawa, an endometrial cancer cell line) cultured in the absence of oestradiol. No PR expression was induced by camizestrant in concentrations of 300 nM or 100 nM in MCF7 or Ishikawa cells. Lack of response in the Ishikawa cell line furthermore indicated a reduced risk of uterotrophic agonism in vitro. Furthermore, the lack of agonistic effect of camizestrant was also confirmed in the NHR panel assay, where no significant agonist activity was observed in the tested receptors (&lt; 2% agonism relative to the control agonist). Antiproliferative effect of camizestrant: Camizestrant inhibited cell proliferation in the ER-positive breast cancer cell lines MCF7, CAMA1, T47D and HCC1428 at IC50 values in the low nanomolar range of 0.3 to 0.8 nM in a 7-day cell proliferation study using a sytox green assay for determining the number of dead cells. It was noted that cells were not treated with oestradiol and hence only limited cell growth is expected. However, an IC50 of 4.8 nM was seen in a 4-day sytox green proliferation assay using MCF7 WT cells in the presence of oestradiol (0.1 nM), whereas an IC50 of 33.3 nM was noted in MCF7 Y537S mutated cells without oestradiol. Camizestrant was &gt; 400-fold less potent at inhibiting proliferation in the ER-negative cells lines Calu-6 and A375 with IC50 values of &gt;14422.5 and &gt;17367 nM, respectively. An antiproliferative effect with a potency within the nanomolar range was supported in MCF7 models engineered by CRISPR to express the most prevalent ESR1 mutations (D538G, Y537S, E380Q, Y537N/C/D, L536H/P/R and S463P), all gain of function mutations that confer constitutive ER activity in the absence of oestradiol. Camizestrant inhibited proliferation with IC50 values ranging from 0.15 to 8.71 nM in all these models.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Two in vitro studies were conducted exploring the effect of camizestrant in combination with CDK4/6i on anti-proliferative effect (#ONC9833-010) and expression of downstream pathway biomarkers (#ONC9833-011). In the first study, MCF7 and T47D cells were exposed to increasing concentrations of CDK4/6 inhibitors (i.e. abemaciclib, palbociclib or ribociclib) in the presence or absence of a fixed dose of camizestrant. Enhanced anti-proliferative activity was observed in the presence of camizestrant for all three CDK4/6 inhibitors across a range of concentrations.

The second study was conducted in MCF7 cells to evaluate the effect of camizestrant and CDK4/6i combination-treatment on modulation of ER and CDK4/6 pathway biomarkers by western blotting, as well as on cell cycle by EDU and DNA staining followed by flow cytometry. Results were not highly convincing but some trends could be seen. Camizestrant caused a dose-dependent reduction in ER and the ER-regulated gene for the TFF1 protein which was maintained in the presence of the CDK4/6i, indicating that CDK4/6i did not impact the ability of camizestrant to inhibit and degrade ERα. Phosphorylated RB (pRB) S780 levels, a cell cycle marker for G1/S transition and hence cell proliferation, was dose-dependently reduced by each monotherapy but even more by the combination of camizestrant and the different CDK4/6 inhibitors. This correlated with cell cycles data indicating a dose-dependent arrest in G1, which increased with combination-treatment especially at the higher concentrations tested (&gt;100 nM CDK4/6i and 30 nM camizestrant). Similar tendencies were seen in the mutated MCF7 ESR1 Y537S cells.

Antiproliferative effect of camizestrant in combination with CDK4/6i: In vitro cellular activity of the glucuronide metabolites of camizestrant:

The major drug-related circulating metabolites of camizestrant in human plasma were the Nglucuronide M4 (AZ14191353) (accounting for 20.1% in humans) and the N-glucuronide of an acid metabolite M14 (AZ14484678) (accounting for 11.2% in humans). Two in vitro studies were conducted in order to evaluate potency and extent of ERα degradation induced by M4 (AZ14191353) and M14 (AZ14484678) using a plate-based immunofluorescence assay or a HTRF assay in ER-positive MCF7 cells. N-glucuronide M4 (AZ14191353) degraded ER to the same extent as camizestrant but with a much lower potency (approximately 500-fold lower), as seen by pIC50 values of 10 and 7.3 for camizestrant and M4, respectively. Conversely, M14 appeared to be inactive at ERα (pIC50 &gt;5). Potential metabolite off-target effects were not addressed in these studies.

## 4.3.1.1.2. In vivo pharmacology

## Pharmacodynamic and -kinetic properties of camizestrant:

The pharmacodynamic and -kinetic properties were evaluated in human cell line xenograft and patient derived xenograft (PDX) mouse models expressing respectively mutant (CTC174 DG538G ESR1m) and wildtype (MCF7 ESR1wt) forms of the receptor in order to assess the effect of camizestrant on ERα protein levels or progesterone receptor (PR) levels, a protein whose expression is regulated by ER. In the PDX ESR1 mutant model female NSG mice were administered a single oral dose of 0.1, 0.8 or 10 mg/kg camizestrant. Blood and tumour tissue were sampled at 2, 5, 8 and 24 h for PK analysis (plasma concentration) and immunoblotting. Likewise, in the wildtype xenograft model, male SCID mice supplemented oestrogen, were treated orally for 3 days with 0.5, 2 or 10 mg/kg camizestrant

and samples were taken at 24, 72 and 120 h.

Generally, a time and dose-dependent effect of camizestrant on ERα degradation or PR protein levels in tumour tissue were shown in mice xenograft models expressing mutant and wildtype forms of the receptor, respectively. In the ESR1 mutant PDX model, the highest dose of 10 mg/kg was required to achieve &gt; 80% ERα degradation that was maintained for 24 h. Lowering the dose, reduced both the extent and duration of ERα degradation. Likewise, in the wildtype model, an effect on PR protein levels was noted by a significant reduction in expression levels of 89% and 93% at doses of 2 mg and 10 mg/kg at 24h. The significant reduction was maintained for the 10 mg/kg dose group for 72 and 120 h (85% and 46% reduction, respectively), whereas PR levels were only significantly reduced up to 72 h for the 2 mg/kg group (47% reduction). In vivo effect of camizestrant monotherapy: In vivo anti-tumour efficacy of camizestrant was shown in a number of ESR1 wild-type and ESR1 mutant ER+ breast cancer cell line and patient derived xenograft (PDX) models in immunocompromised mice (wt MCF7, MCF7 Y537S ESR1m, CTC174 D538G ESR1m, oestrogen independent HBXF079\\_LTED ESR1m, CTG1211 D538G ESR1m and ST941 Y537S ESR1m). The animals were orally dosed daily in up to 42 days (21 to 42 days). In initial models, a number of doses were tested (from 0.5 up to 50 mg/kg), whereas later models were conducted with doses of 10 mg/kg. The dose of 10 mg/kg was established as the most suitable dose, as it generally provided significant better antitumour efficacy compared to lower doses and higher doses did not prove marked superiority. Across all models, in vivo anti-tumour efficacy was evident as significant tumour growth inhibition (TGI) compared to vehicle control and, in most models, also tumour regression. Anti-tumour activity was dose-dependent and generally most significant at doses of 10 mg/kg, and at this dose tumour regression also occurred in the majority of models. Additionally, ERα and/or PR protein levels was measured in the tumour tissue and reduction in levels appeared to correlate with anti-tumour efficacy. Mathematical PK/PD/efficacy modelling based on the CTC174 DG538G ESR1m data confirmed ERα as a biomarker for tumour growth and indicated that 60% ERα degradation leads to 50% tumour growth inhibition, while tumour stasis requires 90% ER degradation. Additionally, camizestrant monotherapy was tested at oral doses of 10 mg/kg for up to 60 days across a panel of 28 ER-positive breast cancer patient derived xenograft (PDX) models in different strains of mice. The models were established from primary or metastatic biopsies, containing both ESR1 wildtype and mutant forms of the receptor. Significant tumour growth inhibition (TGI&gt;75%) compared to control were noted in 16/28 (57%) of the models, with tumour regression in 7 of those models. When overlaying the ESR1m status of the 16 sensitive models, 8 of them were ESR1wt and 8 contained an ESR1 mutation. Activity was seen in models conducted from patients that had previously been exposed to endocrine therapies (ET) plus a CDK4/6i and from patients with acquired palbociclib resistance. In vivo effect of camizestrant in combination with CDK4/6i therapy: In initial studies, the combination of camizestrant (10 or 40 mg/kg) and the CDK4/6 inhibitors palbociclib or abemaciclib (50 mg/kg) were tested in a number of wildtype and mutant PDX mouse models (i.e. CTC174 D538G ESR1m, HBXFO79-LTED ESR1wt and treatment naïve ST3632 ESR1wt). In all models, a significant increased anti-tumour efficacy compared to the individual monotherapies was noted for the combinations, and in some models also tumour regression. eight ER-positive breast cancer PDX models including both wildtype and ESR1 mutants (CTC174, CTG- Superiority of combination treatment with CDK4/6i's were furthermore confirmed across a panel of 1211, CTG-3266, CTG-2432, ST2056, ST3164BPBR, ST1799PBR and ST941HIPBR) treated with camizestrant (10 mg/kg) and/or the CDK4/6i's abemaciclib (50 mg/kg), palbociclib (50 mg/kg) or ribociclib (75 mg/kg) either as monotherapies or in combination. Compared to camizestrant monotherapy, which showed anti-tumour efficacy in 6/8 PDX models, the combination of camizestrant with the three CDK4/6i significantly improved tumour growth inhibition in 4/8 for abemaciclib, 3/8 for palbociclib and 4/6 for ribociclib. In the 4 models where camizestrant monotherapy drove tumour stasis or regression, the combination treatment still drove equivalent or greater results. A mathematical Bayesian mixed-effect model confirmed superiority for the combination-treatments compared to monotherapies and indicated that efficacy was not affected by the choice of CDK4/6i.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A 21-day dose-response combination study in an ER-positive MCF7 xenograft male mouse model combining camizestrant doses of 1, 3 or 10 mg with ribociclib doses of 37.5 or 75 mg/kg, showed that camizestrant inhibited tumour growth in an exposure dependent manner, both as a monotherapy and in combination with ribociclib. Furthermore, a greater anti-tumour efficacy was noted for the combination, which was also associated with more pronounced inhibition of pRB1 (target of CDK4/6i) when measuring the cell cycle biomarker with western blot in tumour tissue. Whereas, PR levels in tumour tissue appeared to be more related to camizestrant alone with a dose-dependent relationship. 4.3.2.1. It was noted that the combination with ribociclib, noticeably increased camizestrant exposure in plasma from 2 to 5-fold at the highest and lowest dose, respectively. See also section 4.3.2.1. for more details. This was further addressed in the clinical part of the dossier, where data indicated the other CDK4/6i's (i.e. abemaciclib and palbociclib) did not cause similar increases in camizestrant exposure (see 5.2. ). Additionally, an exposure/efficacy analysis confirmed that the increased camizestrant exposure was not sufficient to explain the improved efficacy seen for the combination treatment in the non-clinical dose-response study.

## In vivo ER agonism:

The utertrophic effect of camizestrant was explored in vivo to exclude a risk of endometrial cancer in patients as seen for the selective ER modulator tamoxifen due to partial ER agonism in uterine tissue (Fisher et al, 1995). Lack of utrotrophic agonistic effect of camizestrant was shown in a 3-day repeatdose study in juvenile Han Wistar rats. Treatment with the known partial ERα agonist tamoxifen (10 mg/kg PO) caused a significant increase in uterine weight compared to vehicle control, whereas both camizestrant (50 mg/kg PO) and fulvestrant (5 mg/kg SC, negative control) significantly reduced uterine weight. This indicated a lack of ERα uterotrophic agonism for camizestrant under the current study conditions.

Common to all the conducted in vivo animal studies, no in-life signs of toxicity or significant reductions in body weight were observed.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

In order to explore secondary off-target pharmacology, camizestrant was screened in a panel of in vitro radioligand binding, enzyme and functional assays, covering 195 distinct molecular targets (receptors, ion channels, transporters and enzymes). Activity was detected for 31 targets as a defined Ki, IC50 or EC50 value. Apart from ERα and β, no targets had activity within 100-fold of the ERα primary target or within the clinically relevant maximum unbound plasma concentration of 0.037 µM obtained at 75 mg. It was, however, noted, that only a 9-fold difference existed in binding Ki values for the H3 histamine receptor and the clinically relevant maximum unbound plasma concentration. The H3 histamine receptor primary regulates neurotransmitter release in the brain and CNS. However, no neurological issues were detected in the toxicity studies or in a modified Irwin screen in rats conducted as part of the safety pharmacology program. Hence, despite the relatively small fold change for antagonistic effect on the H3 histamine receptor in vitro, no clear correlation was seen to relevant in vivo findings. Additionally, an in vitro agonistic effect on the µ opioid receptor were noted with a 116fold change for binding Ki and 257-fold for IC50 compared the clinically relevant unbound Cmax of 0.037 µM.

Moreover, camizestrant was screened in an in vitro selective voltage-gated cardiac ion channel electrophysiological assay at concentrations up to 33.3 µM (#4428SV) and no activity was noted at any of the channels tested, including the hHCN4 channel. Hence, IC50&gt; 33.3 µM existed for all the tested channels (&gt;892-fold to clinically relevant unbound Cmax). Nevertheless, an effect of camizestrant on HCN-channels appeared relevant in vivo with respect to toxicity findings in the heart and eye (see Section 4.4.8 on mechanistic studies).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

A core battery of safety pharmacology studies was conducted to evaluate the effect of camizestrant on the central nervous, cardiovascular and respiratory system in accordance with ICH S7A and S7B guidelines. Additional non-GLP follow-up and supplementary studies were conducted to further evaluate the cardiovascular effects of camizestrant.

## 4.3.1.4. . Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

Pharmacodynamic drug interactions were assessed in the clinical efficacy section, see 5.2.3. .

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

Similar to humans, exposure in rats and dogs increased greater than dose-proportional after repeated dosing based on AUC, and a high volume of distribution is observed indicating high distribution to tissues. Non-linear kinetics are observed in both humans and dogs as increasing dose levels led to a decrease in plasma clearance and volume of distribution. Oral bioavailability in rats, dogs and humans were 22%, 39% and 43%, respectively. While a large difference in plasma exposure was observed between male and female rats with up to 4.5-fold higher exposure in females, no sex difference was observed in dogs. Additionally, an exposure/efficacy analysis confirmed that the increased camizestrant exposure was not sufficient to explain the improved efficacy seen for the combination treatment in the non-clinical dose-response study. No dedicated PK studies with co-administration of CDK4/6 inhibitors were conducted, but a PD study showed increased plasma concentrations when camizestrant is co-administered with ribociclib. It was noted that the combination with ribociclib, noticeably increased camizestrant exposure in plasma from 2 to 5-fold at the highest and lowest dose, respectively. This was further addressed in the clinical part of the dossier, where data indicated the other CDK4/6i's (i.e. abemaciclib and palbociclib) did not cause similar increases in camizestrant exposure (see section 5.2. ).

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

The free percentage of camizestrant in plasma in vitro was determined at 24.7% in humans compared to 15.8% in dogs and 29.3% in rats. Rapid and widespread distribution was observed to all tissues in albino rats with the highest distribution to uveal tract, liver, adrenals, pancreas, spleen, lung and glandular tissues. Tissue levels were overall reversible, however, with higher retention in thyroid, pituitary and uveal tract at the end of the study. Very high amounts were still present in the uveal tract at the end of the study in pigmented rats, indicating an affinity of camizestrant to melanincontaining tissue, which may correlate with observed ocular toxicity. A very low distribution across the blood-brain barrier was observed. While placental transfer was not specifically investigated, developmental toxicity findings suggest that foetuses are exposed to camizestrant via the placenta (see Section 4.4.5).

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

Identified metabolites of camizestrant were either oxidative metabolites or glucuronides of oxidative metabolites. Large variation was observed in metabolism pattern between male and female rats and between rats and dogs, where the metabolism profile in dogs seems to be more comparable to humans. Two major metabolites (i.e. above 10%) were observed in human plasma which were not found in rat and dog plasma; M4 (AZ14191353, an N-glucuronide conjugate of camizestrant) and M14 (an N-glucuronide of an acid metabolite). Oxidative metabolism products in human plasma were also observed in animals and glucuronide metabolism products are of low concern due to high polarity. It was documented in vitro that the human glucuronides M4 and M14 are considered stable at pH 7.4 and thus pose no risk of chemical reactivity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Excretion studies showed that fecal elimination was the main route of excretion of camizestrant and its metabolites in all species after oral and IV administration, with renal excretion only constituting a minor route. Human excretion data suggested that biliary excretion of camizestrant and its metabolites occurs prior to fecal excretion and data from BDC rats and dogs supports this. Excretion in milk in nonclinical species was not investigated.

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

Pharmacokinetic drug interactions are assessed in the clinical safety (see Section 5.4.10).

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

In dogs, camizestrant inhibited the metabolism of atropine, which resulted in higher atropine plasma concentrations.

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

Dedicated single dose toxicity studies have not been conducted for camizestrant, in line with ICH M3(R2) 2009 and ICH S9 2010 guidance, but information on acute oral toxicity is available from other studies that have defined the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) following short term dosing in mice (20377186), rats (BE001738-15 and 505104) and dogs (505120). Acute oral toxicity of camizestrant in mice, rats and dogs was dose-dependent, with reduced tolerability at high doses leading to premature termination in rodents (150 mg/kg/day in rats and 300 mg/kg/day in mice) and dogs (≥40-50 mg/kg/day), associated with decreased activity, body weight loss and neurological or other adverse clinical signs. At lower dose levels, toxicity was limited, with no mortality in rodents and generally good tolerability in dogs at 10-20 mg/kg/day. In dogs, the maximum tolerated dose was 20 mg/kg/day for 14 days, with higher exposures resulting in CNS-related toxicity and early euthanasia. Treatment-related findings in dogs included cardiovascular effects (decreased heart rate with QT/QTc prolongation) and reproductive organ changes consistent with oestrogen receptor antagonism.

## MOUSE

In mice, repeat dose toxicity was characterised by dose dependent effects from ≥25 mg/kg/day, with a non-tolerated dose of 300 mg/kg/day. No NOAEL was established. The most sensitive targets were the reproductive organs, immune system, and liver, often occurring at exposures with limited margins to clinical exposure.

Marked reproductive tract changes were observed in females from ≥25 mg/kg/day, including ovarian haemorrhagic follicles, reduced corpora lutea, follicular cysts, and epithelial atrophy of uterus, oviduct, and vagina. These findings were associated with adrenal cortical atrophy and pituitary hypertrophy. In males, Leydig cell hypertrophy was observed.

Liver findings included hepatocellular vacuolation and hypertrophy, supported by increases in liver enzymes and alterations in protein and lipid parameters, indicative of functional hepatic effects.

Immunosuppressive effects were evident from ≥25 mg/kg/day, with lymphopenia, reduced white blood cell counts at higher doses, thymic atrophy, reduced spleen weight, and lymphoid depletion. Other findings included increased heart and kidney weights, alveolar macrophage accumulation in lungs, and renal epithelial changes. At non tolerated doses, multi organ degenerative changes were observed, consistent with systemic toxicity.

## RAT

In rats, toxicity was evaluated in studies up to 26 weeks, with a non-tolerated dose of 150 mg/kg/day. The heart and reproductive systems were the principal targets, with a consistent profile across studies. Females had higher systemic exposure than males (up to 4.5-fold), contributing to greater sensitivity.

Reproductive toxicity was a prominent finding. In females, adverse effects occurred from ≥0.1 mg/kg/day (no NOAEL), and in males from ≥10 mg/kg/day (NOAEL 5 mg/kg/day). Females showed widespread changes in reproductive tissues including ovarian cysts and tumours, adrenal cortical atrophy, mammary hyperplasia, and uterine/vaginal epithelial atrophy, with necrosis and inflammation at higher doses. In males, there was degeneration and atrophy of testes and accessory glands, reduced sperm counts and motility, and impaired fertility, with marked effects at higher doses.

Hepatobiliary toxicity consistent with cholestasis was observed, particularly in females, with biochemical changes from ≥10 mg/kg/day and histopathological findings at higher doses. These effects were largely reversible after treatment cessation.

Phospholipidosis and macrophage aggregation were observed in lungs and lymphoid tissues from ≥10 mg/kg/day, confirmed ultrastructurally and considered treatment-related.

Cardiovascular findings included progressive cardiomyopathy in females at ≥25 mg/kg/day. Additional effects included altered body weight and food consumption, with sex-dependent patterns over long-term dosing.

## DOG

In dogs, repeat-dose toxicity was assessed up to 39 weeks, with a non-tolerated dose of 40 mg/kg/day and a maximum tolerated dose of 20 mg/kg/day (14 days). The cardiovascular and reproductive systems were the primary targets, with no NOAEL established.

Cardiovascular toxicity was a key finding, occurring from ≥5 mg/kg/day (~3.5-4fold human exposure). Effects included bradycardia, QT/QTc prolongation, PR interval shortening, and blood pressure changes, with delayed onset and partial reversibility. QT prolongation was independent of heart rate changes, suggesting a direct electrophysiological effect. These findings are consistent with nonclinical pharmacology indicating HCN channel inhibition and are considered of potential clinical relevance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Reproductive toxicity affected both sexes from ≥5 mg/kg/day. In females, findings included uterine and vaginal atrophy, cystic endometrial hyperplasia, inflammation, and ovarian cysts and neoplastic changes (granulosa cell tumours and stromal hyperplasia). In males, testicular and epididymal degeneration, Leydig cell changes, impaired spermatogenesis, and pituitary cysts were observed. These findings indicate disruption of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis and are considered adverse. Additional observations included transient visual effects (linked to retinal ion channels), hormonal changes consistent with central endocrine modulation (TSH suppression with unchanged T4), and limited CNS findings at high exposures.

## Cross-species findings

Across all species, reproductive and endocrine findings (including adrenal and pituitary changes) were consistent with pharmacological ER antagonism.

Cardiovascular effects (heart rate reduction and QT prolongation) were observed in rats and dogs and supported by mechanistic studies demonstrating HCN4 channel involvement.

Phospholipidosis and macrophage accumulation were identified in rats and mice, primarily at high exposures, and were generally reversible.

Overall, repeatdose toxicity of camizestrant is characterised by dose-dependent, pharmacologically driven effects on reproductive, cardiovascular, and endocrine systems, with additional multiorgan effects at higher exposures.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

Camizestrant was negative in a standard battery of genotoxic tests and thus appears to be without genotoxic potential (Table 10).

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

No carcinogenicity studies have been performed for the current procedure in accordance with ICH S9. Findings in the ovaries in chronic repeat-dose studies included hyperplasia at total plasma exposures (AUC) ≥ 2-fold (rat granulosa cell) and 52-fold (dog sex cord stromal) relative to human exposure at MRHD and benign granulosa cell tumours at total plasma exposures (AUC) of approximately 60-fold (rats) and 3.5-fold (dogs) relative to human exposure at MRHD. An interstitial (Leydig) cell adenoma was observed in the testis of a single dog at 13-fold human exposure at MRHD. Findings are considered hormone-driven, based on the pharmacological action of camizestrant and therefore not relevant for post-menopausal women or in pre- or peri menopausal women or men administered an LHRH agonist.

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

Male fertility was assessed as part of the 6-month study in rats (Study 509329) and female fertility was assessed in a stand-alone fertility study (Study 490021). A modified rat embryofoetal development study (Study 490005) was conducted which included a littering phase in which dams received camizestrant from Day 6 of gestation until Day 6 of lactation. Assessment of embryofoetal development was not assessed in a second species.

## 4.4.5.1. Fertility and early embryonic development

In a dedicated fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study evaluating fertility in female rats (Study 490021), 14 days dosing with camizestrant was associated with alterations in oestrous cycles, mating behaviour, and fertility. At the mid-dose of 0.075 mg/kg/day and higher, mating signs were increased while marked reductions in fertility were observed, primarily due to a reduction in oestrous cycles followed by decreased pregnancy rates. From the lowest dose of 0.001 mg/kg/day, fewer implantations and live embryos were detected, accompanied by increased rates of both preimplantation and post-implantation losses. All effects on fertility were fully reversible following a 4week recovery period. No female fertility NOAEL was determined from the study, but effects are likely to have occurred at clinically relevant exposure levels based on extrapolations of exposures values with an estimated safety margin &lt;1 for all findings and far below clinical exposure for pre- and postimplantation losses specifically (&gt;400-fold). Study results are summarised in Table 11.

Effects of camizestrant on male fertility was assessed as part of the GLP 6month repeat-dose study in rats (Study 509329). Relevant histopathology findings were observed in a dose-dependent manner at ≥10 mg/kg/day encompassing atrophy in the prostate gland and seminal vesicle, tubular dilation, degeneration/atrophy of the seminiferous tubules in the testis and reduced sperm cellularity in the epididymis. Sperm evaluation revealed decreased testis spermatid and epididymal sperm counts at ≥10 mg/kg/day, increased abnormal sperm at ≥25 mg/kg/day, and reduced sperm motility at 75 mg/kg/day. Reproductive performance was also affected with a reduction in mating rate (≥25 mg/kg/day) and non-dosed females experiencing a decreased number of live foetuses accompanied by higher implantation loss (75 mg/kg/day) reflecting transferred toxicity from dosed males. No male NOAEL was determined. Effects occurred at the lowest administered dose at exposures corresponding to 2x human exposure with an estimated safety margin &lt;1. Study results are summarised in Table 12.

## 4.4.5.2. Embryo-fœtal development

In a non-standard rat embryofoetal development study (EFD, Study 490005), dosing of 0.1 mg/kg/day to female rats during fallopian transport and implantation led to a full lack of implantation in all females in spite of unaffected mating behaviour. This resulted in a fertility index of 0% and indicates that camizestrant has early pre-implantation effects. Dosing in the range of 0.001 and 0.075 mg/kg/day from the time of implantation GD6, had no notable effects on implantation sites, embryofoetal survival, growth and development. However, when dams were dosed 0.075 and 0.75 mg/kg/day from the point of implantation through lactation on LD 6, camizestrant appeared to have detrimental effects on parturition in the form of difficulties birthing and/or prolonged gestation and parturition with example of remaining pups in utero after completed labour. Importantly, camizestrant led to increased pup mortality and overall deterioration in the well-being of dams, which resulted in unscheduled euthanasia GD 22/23 of all dams/pups at the high dose 0.75 mg/kg/day. Female fertility and pup survival were negatively affected at an estimated safety margin &lt;1.
