## 4.4.5.3. Prenatal and postnatal development, including maternal function

In accordance with ICH S9 (2010), a dedicated pre-natal and post-natal development study is not required for the patient population. However, the modified rat embryofoetal development study included a littering phase in which dams received camizestrant from Day 6 of gestation until Day 6 of lactation (Study 490005), see Table 14.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.5.4. Studies in which the offspring (juvenile animals) are dosed and/or further evaluated

Juvenile studies have not been conducted as the proposed indication is for adult patients.

## 4.4.6. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

Details on pharmacokinetics are provided in the section on Absorption, see 4.3.2.1.

In repeat dose toxicity studies, systemic exposure to camizestrant was generally dose-proportional in mice, slightly greater than dose-proportional in rats (particularly in females also increasing disproportionately to males ≥10 mg/kg/day), and greater than dose-proportional in dogs at higher doses. In humans, for comparison, exposure to camizestrant increased in a greater than dose proportional manner after single and multiple dosing across the 25 to 450 mg dose range (SERENA-1 study).

In rats, systemic exposure increased from low levels at ≤0.01 mg/kg/day to approximately AUC(0-24) ~0.005 µmol·h/L at 0.073/0.091 mg/kg/day and further to ~0.08-0.09 µmol·h/L at 1 mg/kg/day, reaching ~0.86-1.14 µmol·h/L at 5 mg/kg/day, with higher exposures consistently observed in females. At higher dose levels in rats, exposure increased disproportionately, with AUC values up to ~67-182 µmol·h/L at 75 mg/kg/day and up to ~332-535 µmol·h/L in females at ≥150-200 mg/kg/day, together with corresponding increases in Cmax (e.g. up to ~10.4 µmol/L at 75 mg/kg/day in females).

In mice, exposure was generally dose-proportional across the tested range, with AUC values of ~3252 µmol·h/L at 25-50 mg/kg/day and up to ~182-225 µmol·h/L at 150-300 mg/kg/day, without consistent sex differences.

In dogs, exposure also increased more than dose-proportionally at higher doses, with AUC values of ~8-9 µmol·h/L at 5 mg/kg/day, increasing to ~38 µmol·h/L at 10 mg/kg/day and up to ~110-120 µmol·h/L at 20 mg/kg/day, with corresponding Cmax values up to ~8-12 µmol/L.

Following repeated dosing, no accumulation was observed in mice and dogs, whereas in rats exposure increased with repeated administration, particularly at higher doses. No consistent sex differences were observed in mice or dogs, whereas female rats showed higher exposure than males.

Across species, there was no evidence of marked accumulation over time. Tissue distribution studies in dogs showed higher concentrations in the heart compared with brain and plasma, consistent with cardiac tissue as a potential site of accumulation.

## 4.4.6.1.1. Interspecies comparison and exposure margins to clinical exposure

Exposure margins (animal:human) are calculated by comparing exposure parameters (Cmax and AUC) at the No-Observed-Adverse-Effect Level (NOAEL) from toxicology studies with those at a clinical dose of 75 mg once daily (as monotherapy or in combination) and presented in Table 14. When a NOAEL is not available, the Lowest-Observed-Adverse-Effect Level (LOAEL) is used instead, signifying that adverse effects likely occurred below the lowest dose administered.

Data presented are total plasma exposure AUC at steady state. Animal:human exposure margins were calculated to at clinical dose of 75 mg QD. When camizestrant was combined with ribociclib, median AUCtau corresponded to 2749 ng·h/mL or 5.777 μmol·h/L. When camizestrant was used as monotherapy or combined with abemaciclib or palbociclib, median AUCss and Cmaxss corresponded to 1050.9 ng· hr/mL and 71.5 ng/mL or 2.206 μmol·h/L and 0.150 μmol/L, respectively. Human exposure values were based on a pop-PK model. When camizestrant was administered in combination with 400 mg ribociclib, the camizestrant Cmaxss and AUCtau were 2.4-fold and 2.6-fold higher than achieved with monotherapy and, when administered in combination with 600 mg ribociclib, the Cmaxss and AUCtau were 2.8-fold and 3.2-fold higher and comparable with 150 mg monotherapy.

*AUCtau achieved with monotherapy+600 mg ribociclib was chosen for calculations as a worst-case scenario.

Interspecies comparisons were conducted across pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies in mice, rats, and dogs, as well as in stand-alone EFD and FEED studies in rats. Among these, NOAELs were established only for male rats in the 2- and 4-week studies of shorter duration. For most animals, no safety threshold could be defined, and no NOAELs were identified for females in any species across the pivotal studies, indicating that drug-induced toxicity occurred at all dose levels and at clinically relevant exposures in all species evaluated.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Systemic exposure to camizestrant differed across species, with female rats showing higher sensitivity, consistent with pharmacokinetic differences. In rats, systemic exposure (AUC) increased more than dose-proportionally at doses ≥10 mg/kg/day, reaching up to approximately 4.3-fold higher levels in females compared with males, which may have contributed to the greater sensitivity observed in this species.

Findings related to phospholipidosis and macrophage aggregation were observed at high exposure multiples relative to humans, including approximately 30.7-fold and 82.5-fold human exposure in male and female rats, respectively, and ~53-fold in mice. In dogs, similar findings were observed at approximately 58.5-fold human exposure.

Cardiovascular findings in dogs occurred at comparatively lower exposure margins, with a cardiac LOAEL at 5 mg/kg/day corresponding to approximately 3.5-fold human exposure, while ocular findings in dogs were identified at 10 mg/kg/day (approximately 13.8-fold human exposure).

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

Camizestrant is orally administrated and gastrointestinal tract toxicity has been assessed as part of repeat dose toxicity studies, see 4.4.2. No relevant gastrointestinal changes were noted.

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

## 4.4.8.1. Antigenicity

No dedicated antigenicity studies have been conducted for camizestrant.

## 4.4.8.2. Immunotoxicity

No dedicated immunotoxicity studies have been conducted for camizestrant, see 4.4.2. . This aspect is covered by repeat dose toxicity studies in rodents and dog.

## 4.4.8.3. Dependence

Dependence studies have not been conducted in accordance with ICH S9.

## 4.4.8.4. Studies on metabolites

No separate evaluation has been performed with disproportional metabolites. Metabolites have been described under the dedicated section on Metabolism, see 4.3.2.3.

## 4.4.8.5. Studies on impurities

Specified levels of impurities are considered toxicologically qualified.

## 4.4.8.6. Phototoxicity studies

The phototoxic potential of camizestrant was investigated using 3T3 Neutral Red Uptake (NRU) assay and measured by the relative reduction in viability of Balb/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts (GLP Study 20136267) following exposure to camizestrant and ultraviolet radiation (UVR). Promethazine was used as a positive control in the definitive assay. The IC₅₀(light) of camizestrant was comparable to IC₅₀(dark), indicating no phototoxic potential. No reduction was observed in viability of fibroblasts after +UVR. Camizestrant was not phototoxic when tested up to 100 µg/mL.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.8.7. Excipient studies

No studies have been conducted on excipients.

## 4.4.8.8. Other (toxicity) studies (including mechanistic studies)

## 4.4.8.8.1. Mechanistic studies on heart rate reduction

A reduction in heart rate has been observed in nonclinical species and in clinical trials with camizestrant. In vitro and in vivo mechanistic studies related to these findings were conducted to assess the hypothesis that camizestrant-related heart rate reduction is via an HCN channel-mediated mechanism. Studies and their results are described below in Table 15.

At high exposures, abnormal APD90 lengthening at a high concentration indicates additional uncharacterised ion channel involvement.

## 4.4.8.8.2. Mechanistic studies on visual effects

Visual effects have been reported in clinical trials with camizestrant. Three in vivo mechanistic rat studies were conducted to determine if there was a functional effect on the retina (Table 16).

No histological changes were observed as evaluated by light microscopy, supporting a functional effect rather than structural damage to the retina, which is in line with the lack of ophthalmic findings in repeat-dose toxicity studies. Retinal changes occurred at 12 mg/kg/day (5× human exposure) and displayed a delayed onset.

## 4.4.8.10. Determination of Parathyroid Hormone (PTH) in Dog Serum

Parathyroid hormone levels were measures in serum samples (Study 3801030) collected from surviving animals in Week 39 of the 9-month dog study (Study 509313). No significant differences in parathyroid hormone levels were noted between the control and treated groups.

Considering the above data of the definitive hazard assessment, camizestrant is not considered a PBT nor vPvB substance. Camizestrant fulfils the vP criterion. A bioaccumulation potential is not indicated based on the log KOW &lt; 4.5. A definitive PBT/vPvB assessment is not required.

Risk mitigation including statements in relevant sections of the Product information (SmPC and Patient Leaflet) have been included as appropriate.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacodynamics

Several in vitro and in vivo studies were conducted in support of the suggested mode of action (MoA) of camizestrant (AZD9833) showing evidence of estrogen receptor (ERα) binding affinity, specificity and efficacy. Furthermore, literature was presented to demonstrate that human ERα has high sequence homology with the animal species used in the non-clinical program, particularly in the ligand binding domain.

## Primary pharmacology

Camizestrant is a potent and selective oestrogen receptor (ER) antagonist and degrader, targeting both wild-type and ESR1-mutated ERα with IC50's in the low nanomolar range. In vitro, camizestrant inhibited oestradiol-induced signalling and demonstrated antiproliferative activity in ER-positive breast cancer cell lines, including models harbouring ESR1 mutations. The compound showed no evidence of agonistic activity. While camizestrant does not degrade ERβ, it binds to ERβ with high potency. The two camizestrant metabolites M4 and M14 showed respectively low potency or inactivity in terms of ERa degradation in vitro.

In vivo, camizestrant demonstrated dose-dependent antitumour activity in ER-positive xenograft and patient-derived models, including tumour regression. Reduction of ERα levels in tumour tissue correlated with antitumour efficacy and supports the proposed mechanism of action.

Combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors (i.e. abemaciclib, palbociclib or ribociclib) resulted in increased antitumour activity compared with monotherapy, consistent across different inhibitors. While camizestrant markedly improved efficacy of CDK4/6i, camizestrant activity appeared only to be minimally influenced by CDK4/6 inhibitors. However, it should be noted that the combination with ribociclib, noticeably increased camizestrant exposure in plasma.

No evidence of uterotrophic or agonistic ER activity was observed.

## Secondary pharmacology

No clinically relevant off-target activity was identified at relevant exposure in a broad receptor screening panel or an in vitro selective voltage-gated cardiac ion channel electrophysiological assay. Binding to histamine H3 and µ-opioid receptors was observed at high margins without associated functional effects. Given the high affinity for ERβ, pharmacological activity in non-target tissues cannot be excluded but this is included in the observed safety profile.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Safety pharmacology

Safety pharmacology studies identified a cardiovascular safety concern. In rats and dogs, camizestrant induced effects on cardiac electrophysiology, including QT/QTc prolongation, decreased heart rate (bradycardia), and haemodynamic changes. These findings were more pronounced following repeated dosing and occurred at exposure levels with limited safety margins in dogs. In dogs, repeated administration resulted in consistent electrophysiological effects, including QTc prolongation, reduced heart rate, and occasional arrhythmias (atrial premature complexes), with no clear no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL). These findings were partially reversible but showed delayed onset and persistence after treatment cessation for at least 3 days up to 22 days. Mechanistic investigations indicated that bradycardia is likely mediated via inhibition of HCN4 ion channels. However, QT prolongation was not associated with hERG inhibition, and its underlying mechanism remains insufficiently characterised. Overall, the non-clinical data indicate a treatment-related effect on cardiac repolarisation and conduction with potential clinical relevance, particularly under conditions of prolonged exposure or combination therapy.

No adverse effects of relevance were identified for the central nervous or respiratory system.

## Pharmacokinetics

Camizestrant exhibits moderate oral bioavailability (22-43% across species) and a high volume of distribution, indicating extensive tissue penetration. Systemic exposure increased non-linearly, particularly after repeated dosing. Distribution studies in animals showed widespread tissue exposure, including preferential accumulation in pigmented tissues (notably the uveal tract), while central nervous system exposure was low. Placental transfer is assumed based on developmental toxicity findings. Camizestrant is primarily metabolised through CYP3A4/5-mediated oxidation and UGT1A4-mediated glucuronidation. Significant human-specific metabolites (M4 and M14) were identified but, in accordance with ICH S9, further non-clinical qualification was not considered necessary. Excretion occurs mainly via the faecal route following biliary elimination. In vitro data indicated that camizestrant inhibits several CYP enzymes (including CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4/5), supporting a potential for drug-drug interactions. Increased exposure with co-

administration of ribociclib was observed in non-clinical studies.

## Toxicology

## Repeat-dose toxicity studies

Repeat-dose studies identified as target organs across species the reproductive organs and hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, liver and endocrine glands, heart and eye and finally lung (phospholipidosis-related findings). Reproductive toxicity was observed in both sexes at clinically relevant exposures and is consistent with the pharmacological mechanism of ER inhibition. Findings included gonadal atrophy, altered fertility parameters, and hormone-related tissue changes. In general, these effects were reversible, although some ovarian findings persisted. In rats, hepatobiliary changes and pulmonary phospholipidosis were observed at high exposure multiples and were considered of limited clinical relevance. Isolated cases of rat cardiomyopathy related to treatment were reported with unclear mechanism and may support overall treatmentrelated cardiac disturbances given the general lack of hERG involvement by camizestrant.

In dogs, cardiovascular findings were consistently observed, including bradycardia, QT/QTc prolongation (confirming safety pharmacology findings of delayed onset and persistence off dose), and effects on cardiac conduction. These occurred at exposure levels close to clinical exposure and no clear NOAEL was established. Additional cases of atrial premature complex (APC) episodes were observed at slightly higher exposures. The totality of findings is considered of potential clinical relevance. Genotoxicity Camizestrant was negative in a standard battery of genotoxic tests (in vitro Ames, in vitro micronucleus assay and in vivo micronucleus test in rats). In pivotal repeat-dose studies, proliferative changes in dogs and rats occurred at 2x human exposure with the exception of an interstitial (Leydig) cell adenoma in a single dog at 13.5x human exposure. The observed abnormal proliferative changes in reproductive tissue are likely to be hormone-driven and are expected counterbalanced in the clinic with concomitant antihormonal treatment or by decreased hormonal status in postmenopausal women. Moreover, the majority of findings were fully reversible. Exceptions included benign granulosa cell tumours in the ovaries of rats and dogs, and a single interstitial (Leydig) cell adenoma in one dog, which were observed only in longer-duration studies (26-week study in rat and 39-week study in dog) not including recovery groups for assessment of reversibility. These could be progression stages of initial proliferative changes observed in the 4week study. These findings have been appropriately described in the SmPC. No formal carcinogenicity testing was performed in accordance with ICH S9, which has been considered as accetpable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Carcinogenecity

No carcinogenicity studies have been submitted, which is accepted given the sought indication in accordance with ICH S9.

## Developmental and reproductive toxicology

Female fertility was evaluated in a dedicated fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in rats following 14 days dosing with camizestrant, which showed that treatment was associated with alterations in oestrous cycles, mating behaviour, reduced fertility (primarily due ot a reduction in oestrous cycles) and impaired implantation (fewer implantations and live embryos were detected, accompanied by increased pre- and post-implantation losses). All effects on fertility were fully reversible following a 4-week recovery period. No female fertility NOAEL was determined from the study, and thus effects are likely to have occurred at clinically relevant exposure levels based on extrapolations of exposure values. Effects on male fertility was assessed as part of the GLP 26-week repeat-dose study in rats where related histopathology findings were observed in a dose-dependent manner at ≥10 mg/kg/day. Decreased sperm quality (decreased testis spermatid and epididymal sperm counts, increased abnormal sperm, and reduced sperm motility). Reproductive performance was also affected (mating rate and fertility) with a suggestion to be noted of transmitted reproductive toxicity to undosed female rats at 27x the human exposure. No male fertility NOAEL was determined. Low dose effects occurred at the lowest tested dose at 2x human exposure. Fertility findings observed in the 26-week rat study are adequately reported in the SmPC and a statement was included that reversibility of effects on male fertility was not investigated. Embryo-foetal development was assessed in a three-phase modified dose range finding embryofoetal development (EFD) study in rats which included a pre- and postnatal assessment spanning a dose range of 0.001- 0.75 mg/kg/day. Developmental studies did not show major direct embryofoetal toxicity at tested exposures; however, pre- and postnatal development was adversely affected, including parturition difficulties and increased pup mortality. No NOAEL was determined and dam fertility, gestation and parturition alongside pup survival were negatively affected at &lt;&lt;2.3x human exposure (AUC exposure at the highest dose for embryo-foetal development was far below human exposure). Based on its mechanism of action, camizestrant is expected to adversely affect reproductive function and is contraindicated in pregnancy and during breastfeeding. Camizestrant carries risk to impair milk production (Rusidze et al., 2021; Jin et al., 2024; Hannan et al., 2023; Kansra et al., 2005; Saji et al., 2000), therefore in line with CHMP Guideline on Risk Assessment of Medicinal Products on Human Reproduction and Lactation (EMEA/CHMP/203927/2005) and with the SERDs approved for treatment of pre-, peri- and postmenopausal women, camizestrant contraindication for breastfeeding is considered adequate. Considering camizestrant's therapeutic indication and the intended patient population as well as the concurrent treatment with a LHRH agonist or antagonist in pre- and perimenopausal women, the wording in section 4.6 of the SmPC is acceptable for use/exposure in women of childbearing potential and during pregnancy as well as the recommended contraceptive measures in women and men. Although adverse clinical observations related to the central nervous system were observed in a male dog following a single dose of 50 mg/kg and in one male and one female dog following 4 daily repeat doses at 40 mg/kg/day these findings were observed at a total maximum plasma concentration above 50x human exposure. Therefore, it was considered acceptable not to have conducted dependence related studies. In addition, nonclinical studies of abuse liability are generally not warranted under ICH S9 guidance (ICH S9 2010).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Phototoxicity

No phototoxic potential was identified.

## Mechanistical studies

Mechanistic investigations for heart and eye confirmed:

- HCN4 channel inhibition as a key driver of bradycardia;
- Likely involvement of HCN channels in ocular effects (functional retinal changes without structural damage).

Mechanistic studies also revealed that camizestrant has in vitro preference for both HCN1 and HCN4 over HCN2, supporting some functional effects in retina and heart. Given that camizestrant engages multiple members of the HCN ion channel family, which show distinct tissue distribution, it cannot be excluded that other toxicities are mediated by off-target HCN inhibition.

## Heart

While it is acknowledged that the mechanistic studies have demonstrated how camizestrant may act, at least in part, through HCN4 ion channels to reduce heart rate, other key cardiovascular findings in safety PD studies and in three repeat-dose studies in dog have not been mechanistically characterised, namely the lengthening of QTc interval. The main non-clinical concern is that the totality of evidence indicates sustained disturbances in cardiac electrophysiological balance across species that may worsen over time and with repeated dosing. This risk may be further amplified in the clinical setting, where co-medications are expected, including agents that independently increase the risk of arrhythmias or QTc prolongation. Of particular concern is the absence of clear mechanistic understanding for several findings. This uncertainty is compounded by the lack of precedent for similar findings among marketed SERDs (to current knowledge), suggesting that the observed cardiac profile may represent a compound-specific risk rather than a class effect. From a non-clinical perspective, a cumulative cardiac risk cannot be ruled out and therefore cardiac findings have been reflected in the SmPC section 5.3. The risk of bradycardia in humans has been acknowledged and implemented in the SmPC as well as will be followed up by routine pharmacovigilance measures.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Eye

The functional electroretinography (ERG) changes observed in rats in the mechanistic studies on visual effects are consistent with temporary visual disturbances observed in the clinical trials-such as photopsia, blurred vision, and impaired vision-arising from rod bipolar cell dysfunction, as demonstrated by the Applicant. Rod bipolar cell dysfunction could be mediated in part by camizestrant's inhibition of HCN1 ion channels expressed by photoreceptors in the inner segments of the retina. However, the ERG pattern observed is not strongly consistent with classic HCN1 inhibition alone as reduced oscillatory potentials in rats suggest additional inner retinal mechanisms contribute to the mixed ERG response. Ocular effects in rats displayed a delayed onset. In humans, the median time to onset of the first visual effect was 8 days, supporting delayed effects as mirrored in the cardiac changes. The ocular toxicity risk in humans has been reflected in the SmPC.

## Impurities

Despite the exemption according to the ICHS9, the classification and control of the potential genotoxic impurities have been carried out in accordance with ICH M7. Specified levels of impurities are considered toxicologically qualified. Dedicated studies on single-dose toxicity, carcinogenicity, local tolerance, antigenicity, immunotoxicity, dependence, metabolites and excipients have not been performed which has been considered acceptable.

## Environmental risk assessment (ERA)

Camizestrant is not PBT nor vPvB, but vP. Camizestrant is not expected to pose a risk to the STP, surface water, groundwater and sediment compartment based on the prescribed use of camizestrant. Camizestrant is not expected to pose a risk to the environment via secondary poisoning. Camizestrant should be used according to the precautions stated in the SmPC in order to minimize

any potential risks to the environment

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

The PD findings support the potential benefit of camizestrant for patients with ESR1 mutations in the therapeutic indication.

The non-clinical data package evaluating the pharmacology and toxicity of camizestrant is considered acceptable to support the marketing authorisation.

In general, the findings in toxicology studies are related to the pharmacological action. Relevant information has been reflected in section 5.3 of the SmPC.

The overall toxicity profile of camizestrant is largely consistent with that of two marketed SERDs (elacestrant and fulvestrant)), however, cardiovascular and ocular toxicities appear unique to camizestrant, and some events are demonstrated to be mediated through the HCN ion channel family. Cardiovascular toxicity remains the non-clinical key area of concern and associated risks have been reflected in Section 5.3 of the SmPC.

## 5.1. Introduction

## 5.1.1. Good Clinical Practice (GCP) aspects

The clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the Applicant.

The Applicant has provided a statement to the effect that clinical trials conducted outside the community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

Based on the review of clinical data, CHMP did not identify the need for a GCP inspection of the clinical trials included in this dossier (see section 2.4.3. ).

## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.1. Methods

Bioanalytical methods for the determination of camizestrant in human plasma and urine were developed and validated (data not shown).

## Other analytes

Full validation reports and amendments were provided for for bioanalytical methods of compounds coadministered with camizestrant including abemaciclib, ribociclib, palbociclip and midazolam.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

The pharmacokinetics of camizestrant were investigated in 8 Phase 1 studies in patients (SERENA 1, D8530C00006, D8530C00007) as well as in healthy post menopausal female participants (D8532C00002, D8532C00003, D8530C00004, D8532C00006), including an ADME study (D8532C00005) also in healthy post menopausal female participants. Camizestrant PK parameters were also investigated in 2 phase II studies (SERENA-2, SERENA-3) and 1 phase III study (SERENA6).

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.2.2.1. Population pharmacokinetics

A PopPK model for camizestrant was developed based on data from 7 clinical studies, SERENA-1 (D8530C00001), SERENA-2 (D8530C00002), SERENA-3 (D8530C00003), Japan PK study (D8530C00006), China PK study (D8530C00007) and SERENA-6 (D8534C00001).

The final PopPK model for camizestrant was a 2-compartment model with three transit compartments and first-order elimination. The model was based on data from camizestrant monotherapy and in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors. Baseline body weight (BBW), ethnicity, concomitant use of ribociclib, and dose were identified as significant covariates of V2/F, and baseline creatinine clearance (BCRCL), concomitant use of ribociclib, and dose on CL/F (Clearance/bioavailability). Figure 1 shows the structure of the camizestrant PopPK model.

Figure 1: Structure of Camizestrant PopPK Model

<!-- image -->

*MTT= (n+1)/Ktr

The parameter estimates for the selected camizestrant final model are reported in Table 21. RSE for typical parameters and random effect was below 30%. Eta-shrinkage for CL/F, V2/F (peripheral compartment/bioavailability) and MTT (mean transit time) were 7.98%, 22.27% and 31.5% respectively. Epsilon-shrinkage was 11.54%. No trends were observed in the presented goodness-of-fit (GoF) plots.

## 5.2.2.2.2. Physiology based pharmacokinetic model

A PBPK model for camizestrant was developed using a combination of in vitro data, human ADME data and clinical pharmacokinetic data following a single 75 mg dose in healthy subjects. The model applied a \"middle-out\" approach and incorporated first-order absorption and mechanistic clearance pathways.

The model was qualified against observed clinical data, showing agreement between simulated and observed plasma concentration-time profiles, as well as key pharmacokinetic parameters (Cmax and AUC) after both single and repeated dosing. Simulations in cancer patients receiving 75 mg once daily reproduced the observed accumulation and exposure profiles described in clinical studies.

The PBPK model further described the impact of CYP3A4 modulation, reproducing the observed increase in camizestrant exposure with strong CYP3A4 inhibition (e.g. itraconazole) and the interaction with CYP3A4 substrates (e.g. midazolam). The camizestrant PBPK model was used to predict DDI potential for camizestrant as a victim when co-administered with CYP3A4/5 inhibitors.

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

## BCS classification

Camizestrant is a di-basic molecule with pKa of 4.8 and 8.4 and logD of 2.3 at pH 7.4. Camizestrant showed high aqueous solubility at pH ranges below neutral (≥1.2 mg/mL in simulated gastric fluid pH 1.2 or acetate buffer pH 4.5 and 1.1 mg/mL in phosphate buffer pH 6.8). Dissolution is fastest at pH 1.2 and decrease with increasing pH. However, dissolution was still &gt;95% after 30 minutes at pH 6.8. Hence, co-administration with PPIs is not expected to impact the degree of absorption. This was confirmed by PopPK modelling where 85 patients (11%) of the population included in the model were using acid reducing agents.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

In vitro, camizestrant was shown to be a substrate of P-gp, but not BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.

| OATP1B1       |                                                              1.0 |                                                           1.5 | Not a substrate |
| OATP1B3       |                                                              0.5 |                                                           1.2 | Not a substrate |

Permeability of camizestrant was evaluated in a Caco-2 cell monolayer permeability assay. The conclusion of the study was that camizestrant has a permeability higher than, or equal to, the high permeability marker minoxidil, but camizestrant permeability is concentration-dependent and subject to efflux at lower concentrations.

Hence, camizestrant may be classified as BCS class 1 compound, although the solubility decreases with higher pH.

## Time for maximal plasma concentration

Camizestrant was administered to both healthy volunteers and patients in single dose Phase 1 studies. In healthy volunteer's full PK profiles are available for doses 75 and 300 mg. Time to maximal plasma concentration was typically between 2 and 6 hours but the range was 1 to 10 hours. There were no obvious differences between the different tablet formulations and the oral solution or whether subjects were fasted or fed. In cancer patients, camizestrant was administered at 25, 75, 150, 300 and 450 mg as a single dose. Here the full range for Tmax was 1 to 24 hours. Following oral administration, camizestrant is absorbed with median peak plasma concentrations achieved between approximately 2 to 4 hours after dosing.

## 5.2.2.4. Bioavailability

The absolute bioavailability of camizestrant was investigated in post-menopausal healthy participants following a single 75 or 300 mg oral dose of camizestrant, administered as the direct compression (DC) tablet, co-administered with a microdose IV administration of [ 14 C]-camizestrant. Camizestrant was rapidly absorbed following administration of the 75 mg tablet, with Cmax observed at approximately 2 to 6 h post-dose. The absolute bioavailability was estimated to be 43% (90% confidence interval [CI]: 36.8%, 49.0%) and 55% (90% CI: 48.5%, 62.5%), respectively, following dosing with 75 and 300 mg camizestrant. Simulations in cancer patients receiving 75 mg once daily reproduced the observed accumulation and exposure profiles described in clinical studies, consistent with the steady state reached by approximately Day 5 once daily dosing and a terminal halflife of ~23 hours.

## 5.2.2.6. Influence of food

At both 75 mg (n = 16) and 300 mg (n = 15), the exposures were similar between the fed and fasted treatments. Following a 75mg dose, the fed to fasted Cmax and AUC geometric mean ratios (90% CI) were 106.2% (90% CI: 94.3%, 119.7%) and 109.8% (90% CI: 104.4%, 115.5%), respectively.

## 5.2.2.7. Distribution

## Volume of distribution

In the relative bioavailability, food effect, and absolute bioavailability study in post-menopausal healthy participants (D8530C00004), the geomean (CV%) Vz for camizestrant was estimated to be 1430 L (15.6%) and 1240 L (40.2%) following a single IV infusion of 100 μg [ 14 C]-camizestrant after a single oral dose of the late phase tablet at 75 or 300 mg, respectively.

Across the dose range 25 to 450 mg administered to cancer patients in SERENA-1, Vss/F decreased from 2563 L to 882 L with dose. At 75 mg, the Vss/F was 1849 L and was similar to the PopPK value of 1888 L (26.97 L/kg).

## Characterisation of metabolites in plasma from the mass balance study (in vivo study D853200005)

Metabolites of camizestrant were detected and quantified in healthy female volunteers (D853200005) receiving a single oral dose of 75 mg [ 14 C]-camizestrant (0.67 MBq). Exposure to camizestrant accounted for 8.4% of circulating plasma TR based on AUC. The geometric mean (CV%) terminal plasma half-life for camizestrant and the TR half-life were 27.06 h (21.4%) and 63.9 h (13.2 %), respectively. The half-life for TR was longer than for camizestrant suggesting any metabolites may have longer terminal elimination.

Figure 11: Concentration versus Time Profiles of Camizestrant and Total Radioactivity in Plasma Following a Single Oral Dose of 75 mg 14 C-Camizestrant in Human ADME Study(D853200005)

<!-- image -->

Vertical lines represent the geomean ± geometric SD.

In the plasma unchanged camizestrant represented 13.6% of the drug related AUC (area under the curve). The main circulating metabolites were M4 (AZ14191353, an N-glucuronide conjugate of AZD9833) and M14 (an N-glucuronide of an acid metabolite) which represented 20.1% and 11.2% of the drug related AUC, respectively. The other nine minor metabolites, each of which had a relative abundance lower than 10% of the total drug-related materials in plasma, were either oxidative metabolites or glucuronides of oxidative metabolites.

In the 14 C-camizestrant hADME study, camizestrant N-glucuronide (M4) and an N-glucuronide of an acid metabolite (M14) showed &gt;10% of drug-related exposure. M4 N-glucuronide metabolite was not detected in rat and dog in vitro hepatocyte experiments or in plasma samples collected from rat and dog toxicology studies or following dosing of 14 C-camizestrant from studies in rats and dogs. M14 was not detected in hepatocytes of human, rat and dog, and was not detected in plasma collected from rat and dog dosed with 14 C-camizestrant. No additional safety studies on M4 or M14 have been conducted.

## Identification of enzymes responsible for the metabolism of camizestrant

By the use of bactosomes prepared from E. coli bacteria which heterologously express individual human CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 isoforms, CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 were found to be the predominant CYP isoforms involved in the metabolism of AZD9833. UGT1A4 was the only UGT showing any significant activity when incubated with camizestrant.

None of the metabolites have had their pharmacokinetics formally characterised.

However, plasma samples from SERENA-1 were analysed for M4. The mean concentration for camizestrant metabolite AZ14191353 (M4) across 4 patients on Day 1 and Day 15 is shown below in Figure 15 M4 represented 20.1% of total radioactivity in plasma.

## 5.2.2.9. Elimination

Across the 25 to 450 mg dose range in SERENA-1, geometric mean (CV%) CL/F decreased with dose from 119.1 L/h (52.28%) to 60.99 L/h (31.63%). At 75 mg across SERENA-1 Parts A+B, Japan Phase I and China Phase I, the CL/F ranged from 70.56 L/h to 102.9 L/h, which was similar to that seen in healthy volunteers (88.8 L/h to 118 L/h) and with the PopPK value (69.1 L/h).

Based on PopPK analysis and data from healthy participants, the terminal half-life is estimated to be approximately 23 h.

## Mass balance study

Following single oral administration of 75 mg [ 14 C]-camizestrant to healthy female participants, an average of 82% of the radioactivity administered was recovered in excreta. The majority of total radioactivity (65.1%) was recovered in faeces indicating faecal excretion was the main route of elimination following oral dosing. In total approximately 17% of camizestrant-related radioactivity was excreted via urine. Approximately 14.5% of camizestrant was excreted unchanged in faeces indicating biliary excretion rather than unabsorbed drug.

Figure 16: Arithmetic mean (±SD) cumulative amount of total radioactivity excreted in urine, faeces and total (urine + faeces) expressed as percentage of dose following a single dose of 75 mg 14 Ccamizestrant.

## 5.2.2.10. Dose proportionality and time dependency

Figure 19: Scatter Plot of Dose Normalised Camizestrant Individual Plasma Cmax and AUC Parameters Following a Single Dose of Camizestrant Monotherapy: SERENA-1 Part A, Cycle 1 Day 15 (PK Analysis Set)

## Cmax/Dose

<!-- image -->

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Boxes are interquartile range (IQR, Q1-Q3), with the median represented by the straight line within the box and the whiskers representing the median +/- 1.5 * IQR, with the open circles representing individual values.

Assessment of dose-proportionality was made using a power model. In Part A of SERENA-1, slope of 1.38 (90% CI: 1.30, 1.46) for Cmax and 1.28 (90% CI: 1.21, 1.35) for AUC(0-24) (estimated following single oral doses) indicated greater than proportional increase in exposures with increasing dose. This was consistent with dose proportionality assessment from the combined data in Part A + B of SERENA-1, (after multiple doses) with slope estimates of 1.24 (90% CI: 1.15, 1.32) for Cmax and 1.18 (90% CI: 1.10, 1.26) for AUCtau (see Figure 19).

Camizestrant is accumulating from single dose to steady state in cancer patients within the range of 1.4 to 2 (SERENA-1). This is according to the dosing interval and the terminal elimination half-life of approximately 23 hours, which would anticipate an accumulation index of 2.

Otherwise, no time dependencies were noted.

Figure 20: Comparison of Camizestrant AUCss as a Monotherapy or in Combination with Palbociclib, Ribociclib or Abemaciclib AUCss

<!-- image -->

The corresponding Cycle 1 Day 15 combination plasma concentrations for abemaciclib, palbociclib, and ribociclib from SERENA-1 were overlaid on published PopPK simulations for the 3 CDK4/6 inhibitors. The steady state abemaciclib, palbociclib, and ribociclib exposures in combination with camizestrant are comparable to the expected PK for each of the CDK4/6 inhibitors, demonstrating that camizestrant did not affect the exposures of abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Inter-individual variability

Across the studies reporting PK parameters in patients and healthy participants dosed with camizestrant, inter-individuel ranged from low (10-20%) to high (&gt;60%). There is no apparent trend of single v. multidose schemes. Intra-individual variability was not studied.

## Therapeutic window

In the SERENA-1 study no MTD or MFD were identified. DLTs were infrequent (3 patients) and occurred only at higher dose levels (≥300 mg). Adverse events related to camizestrant exposure showed a dose-dependent increase from 300 mg onwards, with Grade ≥3 adverse events, serious adverse events, dose reductions, interruptions and discontinuations more frequently observed at 300 mg and 450 mg, resulting in reduced relative dose intensity at these dose levels. With regards to the safety profile at doses ≤150 mg, no Grade ≥3 treatment-related adverse events were reported. The most common treatment-related adverse events were visual disturbances and bradycardia, which were generally Grade 1-2. Bradycardia was observed across all dose levels with a clear dose-dependent trend in heart rate reduction, while visual adverse events increased with dose up to a plateau at higher exposures.

## 5.2.2.12. Special populations

## Renal impairment

No formal study of the PK of camizestrant in subjects with renal impairment was performed based on the mass balance study results. Instead, the PopPK analysis evaluated renal impairment as a covariate. Baseline creatinine clearance was a covariate on CL/F. Patients with mild renal impairment had an exposure increase of 4%-5%, whereas patients with moderate renal impairment had exposure increase of 17-20% compared to a reference baseline of 86.2 mL/min, which was the median of the study. Patients with baseline creatine clearance &gt; 90 mL/min had a reduction in exposure of 9% compared to the reference value of 86.2 mL/min.

The impact of renal impairment on AUCss for cancer patients with impaired renal function is shown in Figure 21 The analysis indicated no clinically meaningful effect of mild and moderate renal impairment at baseline on camizestrant PK, therefore, no dose adjustment is required for participants with mild and moderate renal impairment.

Figure 21: Impact of Renal Impairment on Camizestrant AUCss for Participants on Camizestrant Monotherapy or Camizestrant in Combination with Abemaciclib or Palbociclib, or Camizestrant in Combination with Ribociclib

<!-- image -->

t&gt;

There was a single patient with severe renal impairment and no patients on dialysis included in the clinical studies of camizestrant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Hepatic impairment

The liver is the main organ responsible for clearing camizestrant from the body. Hence a dedicated clinical study (D8532C00002, see Table 20) in subjects with normal (N=8), moderate (N=8) and severe (N=6) hepatic impairment was conducted comparing exposure to 75 mg camizestrant as a single dose.

A dedicated single-dose study was performed in patients with normal, moderate, and severe, hepatic impairment and found that moderate and severe hepatic impairment increaseds camizestrant Cmax (geometric mean) with 2.7-fold and 2.4-fold and AUCinf (geometric mean) with 3.1-fold and 3.8-fold, respectively, while the halflife (geometric mean) increased from 22.43 hours to 28.61 hours and 36.14 hours and CL/F (geometric mean) decreased from 105.8 to 43.72 and 27.98 L/hours, respectively. However, the 90% CI is up to 6.28 for AUCinf, 5.43 for AUClast and 4.71 for Cmax in severe hepatic impairment while the 90% CI is up to 3.87 for AUCinf, 3.57 for AUClast and 4.00 for Cmax in moderate hepatic impairment.

Subjects with mild hepatic impairment were not included in this study as patients in this category were included in the pivotal clinical studies of safety and efficacy feeding into the PopPK model. Hence, mild hepatic impairment was evaluated as a covariate in the PopPK model.

The impact of hepatic impairment on the PK of camizestrant was assessed in post-menopausal female participants with normal, moderate, and severely impaired hepatic function as classified using the Child-Pugh method. Camizestrant exposure was higher in participants with moderate and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B and Class C, respectively), compared with participants with normal hepatic function. Figure 22 shows the concentration - time curve for camizestrant in subjects with normal hepatic function, moderate hepatic impairment and severe hepatic impairment. The statistical comparison is presented in Table 34.

Given that the hepatic impairment dedicated study was not performed at steady state despite camizestrant showing higher than dose-proportional exposure, three different approaches were used to simulate exposure to camizestrant in the three categories at steady state, which were not accepted.

The pooled PopPK analysis included participants with normal hepatic function at baseline (546 participants [72.2%]), mild hepatic impairment (209 participants [27.6%]), and moderate hepatic impairment (1 participant [0.1%]), as defined using the NCI criteria for hepatic impairment. The analysis indicated that there was no statistically significant effect of mild hepatic impairment at baseline on the PK of camizestrant compared to participants with normal hepatic function.

The effect of gender on PK has not been formally investigated during the clinical development of camizestrant as the available and unblinded data to date has been derived only from female participants.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Camizestrant is an oral SERD and complete ER antagonist. It binds to the ligand binding domain of ERα, antagonising the activity of ERα encoded by both wild-type ESR1 and ESR1m which cause constitutive ERα activation, and inducing proteasome-dependent degradation of ERα, without agonising ERα.

Camizestrant was screened in a diverse set of in vitro radioligand binding, enzyme and functional assays, covering 195 distinct molecular targets (receptors, ion channels, transporters and enzymes; Study 4110SV). Apart from estrogen receptors α and β, no targets had activity within 100-fold of the estrogen receptor α primary target. Results of profiling (in vitro and in vivo) of camizestrant against hyperpolarization-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated (HCN) are consistent with the hypothesis that camizestrant-related heart rate reduction is via a HCN channel-mediated mechanism.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

## Evaluation and qualification of models

## Exposure-Response modelling

Cox proportional hazard analysis using SERENA-6 study data indicated no exposure-efficacy relationship at the 75 mg camizestrant dosing level. Exposure-safety relationship was explored graphically using boxplots, and unpaired two-sample t-test was performed for comparison. Exposuresafety analyses were performed independently for SERENA-6 and SERENA-1. Linear logistic regression modelling was performed for analyses, yielding a significant result (p &lt; 0.05). No exposure-safety relationship was indicated for the pivotal study SERENA-6 (camizestrant 75 mg qd). Significant covariates were identified for SERENA-1 study (camizestrant 75 to 300 mg qd). Stepwise covariate analysis indicated that treatment effect from palbociclib had a significant impact on Grade ≥ 1 neutropenia, and treatment effect from palbociclib and baseline neutrophils had a significant impact on Grade ≥ 2 neutropenia and Grade ≥ 3 Neutropenia.

## Concentration-QTc modelling

C-QTc relations of camizestrant was described by a linear mixed-effects model. The estimated population slope (0.019 ms per ng/mL [90% CI: -0.0076 to 0.0456]) and intercept (1.73 ms [90% CI: -2.102 to 5.565]) was not statistically significant. No time delay was indicated between camizestrant concentration and ΔQTcI for 75 mg dose. A slight increase in ΔQTcI was observed between 2 and 8 h post-dose for the 300 mg dose most pronounced in the pre-menupausal patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Effect of camizestrant exposures on QTcI

An evaluation of the effect of 75, 150, and 300 mg steady state camizestrant monotherapy on the QTc interval was performed in Part B of SERENA-1. Continuous 24-h 12-lead ECGs recordings were performed both pre-dose and after repeat camizestrant doses on Cycle 1 Day 15 for the purpose of arrhythmia analysis (including evaluation of mean hourly heart rate), and to allow extraction of replicate 12-lead ECGs at predefined time points. Blood samples were also collected for determining plasma concentrations of camizestrant. Due to camizestrant causing a pronounced effect on HR, data collected during the baseline day (prior to camizestrant dosing) and on-treatment were used to derive individual corrected QTc data (QTcI).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The plotted points denote the pairs of observed drug plasma concentrations and observed ΔQTcI by subjects. The grey dashed line shows the 10 ms threshold.

Camizestrant geomean Cmaxss calculated using non compartimental analysis (NCA) from SERENA-1 data for monotherapy and in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors are presented in Table 43 along with the predicted ∆QTcI. All of the concentrations are below the upper 90% CI predicted concentration (~207 ng/mL) to cause a 10 ms change in QTcI.

As a 600 mg dose of ribociclib is predicted to impact camizestrant PK via a strong, time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4/5, the upper 90% CI ∆QTcI was calculated using the geomean Cmaxss for camizestrant in combination with 600 mg ribociclib as an additional extrinsic factor to evaluate a highrisk clinical scenario. In this scenario, the plasma concentration of camizestrant remained below the threshold concentration of ~207 ng/mL which would result in a 10 ms change in QTcI.

## 5.2.6. Dose selection and therapeutic window

- Non-clinical PK/PD indicated minimal increase in probability of achieving target ER degradation for monotherapy doses greater than 75 mg QD; doses &lt; 75 mg were associated with a substantially lower probability of achieving target ER degradation.
- Comparable inhibition in the biomarkers ER, PgR and Ki-67 for doses from 75 to 300 mg was observed in SERENA-3 in primary breast cancer.
- No statistically significant relationship between camizestrant PK and PFS over the exposure range 75 and 150 mg in the Phase II randomised trial SERENA-2 was observed.
- PopPK analysis indicates no dose adjustment of camizestrant is required on the basis of age, body weight, menopausal status, obesity, albumin, creatinine clearance, or acid reducing agents.
- Ribociclib increases the exposure of camizestrant ~2-fold, consistent with ribociclib being an inhibitor of CYP3A4/5 and camizestrant being a substrate of CYP3A4/5 (itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4/5 inhibitor, increased camizestrant exposure ~2-fold). This ~2-fold increase in camizestrant exposure is comparable with the 2.6 to 3.2-fold increase seen with the observed PK data from the NCA. The Cmaxss and AUCss ratios for 150 mg vs 75 mg camizestrant QD monotherapy in SERENA-1 Parts A and B are 2.6 and 2.5-fold respectively, and therefore greater than 2-fold for a doubling of dose. This reflects the greater than dose proportional increase in exposure with dose. The exposures associated with this increase are comparable to those achieved following 150 mg camizestrant monotherapy.

## 5.2.7. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.7.1. Discussion

## Bioanalytical methods

The bioanalytical method for camizestrant in plasma and urine appear acceptable.

## PK modelling

The final PopPK model was considered fit for purpose to characterise the PK of camizestrant in the patient population, to estimate covariate effects and derive exposure metrics for PK/PD purposes. Statistically significant covariates identified were: baseline body weight (BBW), ethnicity, concomitant use of ribociclib, dose, and BCRCL. GoF and pcVPC plots indicated that the PopPK model was able to capture the central tendency of the observations.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Absorption

Camizestrant can be considered a BCS Class 1 drug. Camizestrant is moderately absorbed and exhibit a bioavailability of 43% at the recommended dose of 75 mg and 55% for a 300 mg dose. This illustrates the magnitude of the non-dose proportional exposure to camizestrant. Despite the relatively good bioavailability, the interindividual variability in exposure was moderate to high. Tmax was highly variable across individuals and independent of dose.

Camizestrant was co-administered with palbociclip and albociclip at dose levels up to 300 mg/day but with ribociclip only at 75 mg due to the irreversible inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 by ribociclip which in general doubled the exposure to camizestrant as compared to monotherapy and co-administered with palbociclip or albociclip.

Formulation differences did not affect bioavailability.

The food effects study showed similar exposures between the fed and fasted states. Therefore, this is reflected in section 4.2 of the SmPC allowing intake with or without food.

## Distribution

The apparent volume of distribution based on PopPK analysis is noted as 26.97 L/kg. In SERENA 1 Vss/F decreased from 2563 L to 882 L with increasing dose.

Camizestrant showed moderate binding to plasma proteins. The unbound fraction of camizestrant in human plasma was 24.7%. No impact of concentration range was observed.

No preferential distribution into blood cells was observed.

## Metabolism

Identification of metabolites in plasma, urine and faeces was performed in a dedicated ADME-study.

In plasma, unchanged AZD9833 represented 13.6% of the drug-related AUC. The main circulating metabolites were M4 (AZ14191353, an N-glucuronide conjugate of AZD9833) and M14 (an Nglucuronide of an acid metabolite) which represented 20.1% and 11.2% of the drug related AUC, respectively. The other nine minor metabolites, each of which had a relative abundance lower than 10% of the total drug-related materials in plasma, were either oxidative metabolites or glucuronides of oxidative metabolites. No active metabolites were identified.

CYP3A4, CYP3A5 and UGT1A4 were identified as responsible for the majority of the metabolism of camizestrant. Thus, the exposure to camizestrant will be impacted by inhibitors and inducers of CYP3A4. The no-effect boundaries of camizestrant are not yet established. However, a conservative proposal could be that -50% to 200% of the exposure observed at the recommended dose of 75 mg is acceptable.

Camizestrant is a reversible inhibitor of CYP3A4, CYP2B6 and an irreversible inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 in vitro. The interaction with CYP3A4 was confirmed in clinical DDI studies.

CYP3A4/5 is not known for clinically relevant genetic polymorphism and is estimated to be responsible for a major part of clearance pathways (51%). UGT1A4 is polymorphic but is estimated to account for only 22% of clearance of camizestrant, therefore no investigations on genetic polymorphism were performed.

Moreover, camizestrant is a substrate of P-gp and an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP and OATP1B1. The information concerning biotransformation in section 5.2 of the SmPC is considered acceptable.

## Elimination

Camizestrant clearance is primarily metabolic and liver-dependent.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Clearance estimated using the PopPK model (69.1 L/h) is referred to in the SmPC.

The terminal half-life of approximately 23 hours is calculated, which was increased with co-administration of ribociclib based on PopPK analysis. The main clearance pathway is through the liver with CYP3A4 being responsible for the majority of the metabolism correlating with the increase in exposure observed when coadministered with ribociclip.

The results of mass balance study indicate that faecal excretion is the major route of elimination, while urinary excretion is the minor elimination pathway: The mean cumulative recovery over the whole collection period (0-432 h) was 82.0 %, with 16.8% being excreted in urine (collected for 216 h) and 65.1% in faeces (collected for 432 h). A somewhat longer sampling time might have been preferable, as the total recovery of radioactivity in urine and faeces should exceed 90% of the dose. Fecally excreted, unchanged camizestrant accounted for 14.5% of the dose. The identified radioactivity was 71.5% in faeces. The majority of faecal recovery seems to be due to biliary elimination and not unabsorbed drug. Active secretion of camizestrant is indicated from the renal clearance which is above blood flow to the kidneys. The identified radioactivity was 67.1% in urine. In plasma, the identified radioactivity was 73.9%. Overall, the percentage identified in the three different matrices is considered sufficient. Camizestrant was present in plasma at 13.6% of the radioactivity. Two glucuronidated metabolites were present in higher amounts (20.1 and 18.2%). All other metabolites were present at levels lower than 10%.

## Dose proportionality and time-dependency

Camizestrant exposure increased in a greater than dose proportional manner over the dose range of 25 to 450 mg, when administered alone (single and multiple dose slope estimates range: 1.18 to 1.38.)

The deviation from dose proportionality is considered modest and not clinically relevant at the recommended dose. Accumulation was limited.

## Pharmacokinetics in the target population

Camizestrant PK was characterised in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors. Exposure was comparable to monotherapy when combined with palbociclib or abemaciclib, while exposure increased with ribociclib. Overall variability was moderate to high. The exposure-efficacy analyses support that 75 mg provides adequate exposure across patients, without evidence of benefit from higher exposures.

## Special populations

No formal study of the pharmacokinetics of camizestrant was performed in subjects with renal impairment, as only a mean of 4.8% of the dose administered in the mass balance study was excreted as unchanged camizestrant in the urine. Renal impairment is not expected to significantly affect PK. This is adequately reflected in the SmPC. Camizestrant is not recommended for participants with severe renal impairment, as safety and PK have not been studied in these participants.

For hepatic impairment, increased exposure and accumulation are expected due to hepatic clearance dependence. Therefore, as a precautionary measure, camizestrant should be avoided in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment. Patients with mild hepatic impairment are allowed to take camizestrant. When a small molecule medicinal product shows higher than dose-proportional exposure, it is recommended that the dedicated study in subjects with hepatic impairment is performed at steady state. In order to mitigate this deficiency, three different approaches were used to simulate exposure to camizestrant in the three categories at steady state which were not accepted. Based on PopPK analyses (n=756), no clinically significant relationships were identified between model predicted steady-state exposure, area under the curve at steady state (AUCss) and the following covariates: baseline median age 61 years (29 to 89 years) and racial groups. PopPK analysis showed baseline median body weight was 69.9 kg (34.2 to 150 kg) and it affected apparent volume of distribution (V2/F), but this was not clinically relevant; it did not significantly impact apparent total body clearance (CL/F) or AUCss. No dose adjustments for camizestrant are required based on age, gender, race, or body weight.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

## In vitro

CYP2B6 and CYP2D6 were reversibly inhibited by camizestrant with CYP2B6 having the potential for in vivo inhibition. As there are no in vivo clinical data the SmPC includes the avoidance of sensitive substrates of CYP2B6. Camizestrant also inhibits CYP2C9 and CYP2C19, supporting precautionary measures in the SmPC. Transporter inhibition (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1) suggests potential interaction risk further supporting precautionary measures in the SmPC.

## In vivo

An increase in midazolam exposure was observed when co-administered with camizestrant indicating that camizestrant is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4/5.

Pretreatment with carbamazepine reduced the exposure to camizestrant. Thus, treatment with a strong CYP3A4/5 inducer decreases camizestrant exposure in a clinically significant manner and the avoidance of strong and moderate inducers is reflected in the SmPC. In patients taking camizestrant in combination with ribociclib moderate inducers are allowed.

As in vitro and mass balance data indicated that approximately 50% of the clearance of camizestrant is through CYP3A4/5, a clinical DDI study with the strong inhibitor itraconazole was conducted. With point estimates below 2 and relatively narrow 90% CIs, the itraconazole study did not indicate that that the exposure of camizestrant was clinically significantly increased by co-administration with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors. However, based on the increase in camizestrant exposure when combined with ribociclib, this combination should be avoided in patients taking additional strong CYP3A4/5 inhibitors. Furthermore, appropriate caution should be exercised with concomitant use of additional weak/moderate CYP3A inhibitors in patients taking camizestrant in combination with ribociclib.

## Pharmacodynamics

Camizestrant showed consistent ER target engagement across dose levels, with similar reductions in ER and proliferation markers.

Apart from the expected activity against estrogen receptors, camizestrant also inhibits HCN ion channels. This is considered to be the likely mechanism behind the AESIs bradycardia and visual effects.

## Exposure-response

No clear exposure-efficacy relationship was identified within the clinical dose range. The available evidence supports that 75 mg achieves sufficient efficacy across exposure ranges. Exposure-safety analyses did not show strong relationships; however, dose-dependent trends for bradycardia and visual effects were observed across studies. QTc analyses did not indicate clinically relevant prolongation at the recommended dose.

## 5.2.7.2. Conclusions

The pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of camizestrant alone and in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors have been sufficiently characterised and reflected in the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose response study

The dose-response of camizestrant was investigated in SERENA-2, a Phase II study in postmenopausal women with advanced ER-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer, which investigated camizestrant as a monotherapy dose daily at 75, 150, and 300 mg. Based on review of the SERENA-1 Phase I camizestrant monotherapy data which revealed no increase in activity with doses above 150 mg, in terms of ORR, CBR24 or PFS, the 300 mg cohort in SERENA-2 was discontinued after enrolment of 20 participants.

Camizestrant produced a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in PFS over fulvestrant at both 75 mg (HR [90% CI]: 0.59 [0.43 - 0.82]; p-value = 0.0177) and 150 mg (HR [90% CI]: 0.70 [0.51 - 0.95]; p-value = 0.0200) camizestrant doses. An increase in both ORR and CBR24 was observed in both the 75 mg and 150 mg camizestrant treatment arms when compared to fulvestrant, demonstrating that a 75 mg dose of camizestrant provided a clinical benefit.

## 5.3.2. Main study

## 5.3.2.1. Study D8534C0001 - Serena-6

## 5.3.2.1.1. Study title

SERENA-6: A Phase III, Double-blind, Randomised Study to Assess Switching to AZD9833 (a Next Generation, Oral SERD) + CDK4/6 Inhibitor vs Continuing Aromatase Inhibitor (Letrozole or Anastrozole) + CDK4/6 Inhibitor in HR+/HER2- mBC Patients with Detectable ESR1 Mutation Without Disease Progression During 1L Treatment with Aromatase Inhibitor + CDK4/6 Inhibitor.

## 5.3.2.1.2. Study design

Phase III, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled study assessing the efficacy and safety of switching to camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor compared with continuing AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor in patients with HR-positive, HER2-negative ABC who develop ESR1 mutations detected in ctDNA using Guardant360 CDx assay during 1L treatment without disease progression.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 38: Study Schema

<!-- image -->

- a. Pre/peri-menopausal females or males (if medically indicated) continued to receive concurrent LHRH agonist/antagonist during STEP 1 (ESR1m Detection Phase). During the STEP 2 (Treatment Phase), they received concurrent monthly LHRH (goserelin or leuprorelin). Dose per local guideline or label.
- b. Patients received the same CDK4/6 inhibitor at the same dose level in STEP 1 and STEP 2.
- c. Patients received the same AI in the STEP 1 and STEP 2.
- d. If routine tumour assessment was performed within 28 days before STEP 2 randomisation and was consistent with RECIST v1.1 standard, no repeat baseline tumour assessment scan was needed. Therefore, it was preferable that tumour assessment during STEP 1 was performed to RECIST v1.1 standards (CT [preferred] or MRI, with IV contrast, of the chest, abdomen and pelvis).
- e. If the patient was followed in the clinic every 2 or 3 months for tumour assessment scans, it was recommended that the ctDNA blood sample collection was taken up to 14 days prior to (or on the same day as) the routine tumour imaging assessment, and ideally in time to allow for the result to be reported to the site in time for the patient's tumour assessment visit.
- f. Once the randomisation cap of about 300 patients was reached and recruitment was closed, patients under surveillance period had the option of providing a ctDNA blood sample for testing at the time of disease progression. Patients who progressed during the surveillance phase were allowed a last ctDNA test at the time of disease progression for detecting the mutation landscape of the tumour.
7. LET, letrozole; ANA, anastrozole

## Randomisation and blinding

Approximately 3050 patients were planned to be screened for ESR1m status (STEP 1: ESR1m detection phase) to allow approximately 300 (in reality 315) patients to be randomised in a 1:1 ratio to one of the following treatment arms (STEP 2: treatment phase):

- Arm A: camizestrant (75 mg oral, OD) + CDK4/6 inhibitor + placebo for AI.
- Arm B: AI (OD) + CDK4/6 inhibitor + placebo for camizestrant.

Randomisation was stratified by:

- Disease site (visceral disease versus non-visceral disease).
- ESR1m detectable at first versus subsequent ctDNA tests.
- Time from initiation of CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI to randomisation (&lt; 18 months versus ≥ 18 months (&lt; 78 weeks versus ≥ 78 weeks)).
- CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib versus abemaciclib versus ribociclib).

All patients were centrally assigned to randomised study treatment using an IRT/RTSM. The kit identification number allocated to the patient at the treatment visit were provided by the IRT/RTSM to the investigator(s) or pharmacists.

The randomisation code was not to be broken except in medical emergencies when the appropriate management of the patient required knowledge of the treatment randomisation. The investigator had to document and report the action to AstraZeneca, without revealing the treatment given to patient to the AstraZeneca staff.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Patient population

The study enrolled adult female (pre/peri-menopausal or post-menopausal) and male patients with advanced ER-positive, HER2-negative breast cancer and who are currently on AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment in the first-line advanced setting. During STEP 1, at the ESR1m Detection Phase, if patients were confirmed to have ESR1m disease, meeting additional screening criteria and, upon confirming no disease progression they were then deemed eligible for STEP 2 phase of the study, where they were randomised to receive study treatment.

## Key STEP 1 Inclusion criteria

## Inclusion Criteria Assessed During Screening for the STEP 1 (ESR1m Detection Phase)

These criteria were to be checked to confirm eligibility prior to a patient signing ICF1 and entering into the study (ie, prior to entry into the ESR1m Surveillance Period), and patients were considered eligible to be included only if all the following criteria apply:

(a) Documentation of ER+ tumour irrespective of PgR status, based on most recent available tumour specimen prior to the screening period utilising an assay consistent with ASCO CAP 2020 guidelines (Allison et al, 2020).

(b) Documented HER2- tumour based on local testing on most recent available tumour specimen prior to the screening period, consistent with ASCO CAP 2018 guidelines (ASCO CAP 2018). Patient must be ≥ 18 years at the time of screening, or ≥ 20 years if in Japan.

- Proven diagnosis of adenocarcinoma of the breast with evidence of locoregionally recurrent or metastatic disease not amenable to resection or radiation therapy with curative intent.
- Documentation of histologically confirmed diagnosis of ER+/HER2- breast cancer based on local laboratory results.
- Currently on treatment and have received ≥ 6 months of AI (letrozole or anastrozole) + CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment as their initial endocrine based treatment for their metastatic disease in concordance with local CDK4/6 inhibitor label or treatment guideline.
- Could have received one prior line of chemotherapy for their locoregionally recurrent or metastatic ER+/HER2- disease before CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI treatment.
- No evidence of clinical 3 or radiological 4 disease progression per investigator assessment.
- Patients must be willing to provide archival radiological images used for tumour assessment at the initiation and/or during CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI for their metastatic disease treatment that support the non-PD assessment, if available, in the event that they have been identified to be ESR1m positive.
- Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group performance status of 0 or 1.
- Minimum life expectancy of at least 6 months
- Minimum body weight of 35 kg
- For those female or male patients who are not abstinent (in line with their preferred and usual lifestyle choice), and intend to be heterosexually active with a partner: female patients must be using highly effective non-hormonal contraceptive measures 5 from the time of screening until 4 weeks after exiting from the ESR1m Detection Phase of the study.Male patients who intend to be sexually active

3 Clinical progression defined as unequivocal evidence of disease progression on clinical grounds in the absence of radiological progression

4 Radiological progression defined as unequivocal progression compared to previous imaging obtained during or prior to initiation of CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI

5 a highly effective method of contraception is defined as one that can achieve a failure rate of less than 1% per year when used consistently and correctly should follow local guidelines

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

with a female partner of childbearing potential must be surgically sterile or using an acceptable method

of contraception. · Pre-/peri-menopausal women and male patients who are currently receiving CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI treatment with concomitant LHRH agonist or antagonist must agree to continue to receive concurrent LHRH agonist or antagonist treatment during the entire ESR1m Detection Phase of the study. Key STEP 1 Exclusion Criteria · Patients who are currently treated with endocrine therapy (AI, tamoxifen or fulvestrant) alone. · No evidence of disease, or bone only disease with sclerotic/osteoblastic bone lesions only (ie, no lytic or mixed lytic + sclerotic lesions) per standard of care imaging. · Have advanced, symptomatic, visceral spread, that are at risk of life-threatening complications in the short term (including patients with massive uncontrolled effusions [pleural, pericardial, peritoneal]), and/or pulmonary lymphangitis. · Known active uncontrolled or symptomatic central nervous system (CNS) metastases, carcinomatous meningitis, or leptomeningeal disease as indicated by clinical symptoms, cerebral oedema, and/or progressive growth. Patients with a history of brain or other CNS metastases or cord compression are eligible if they have been definitively treated with local therapy (e.g., radiotherapy, stereotactic surgery) and are clinically stable off anticonvulsants and steroids for at least 4 weeks before screening phase of the study. · Any evidence of severe or uncontrolled systemic diseases which, in the investigator's opinion, makes it undesirable for the patient to participate in the study or that would jeopardise compliance with the protocol. · Chronic gastrointestinal disease, inability to swallow the formulated product, or previous significant bowel resection that would preclude adequate absorption, distribution, metabolism, or excretion of CDK4/6 inhibitor + AI treatment. · History of another primary malignancy except for the following: (a) Malignancy treated with curative intent with no known active disease ≥ 3 years before STEP 1 (ESR1m detection phase), and of very low potential risk for recurrence. (b) Adequately treated non-melanoma skin cancer or lentigo malignancy without evidence of disease. (c) Other exceptions include basal cell carcinoma of the skin, squamous cell carcinoma of the skin or carcinoma in situ of the cervix that has undergone potentially curative therapy. · Experience of any of the following procedures or conditions in the preceding 6 months: coronary artery bypass graft, angioplasty, vascular stent, any other structural heart disease interventions (eg, cardiac valve repairmen or replacement surgery or transcatheter valve intervention), severe aortic regurgitation (Grade 3/4), myocardial infarction, unstable angina pectoris, cerebrovascular accident, or, transient ischaemic attack. · Congenital long QT syndrome, family history of long QT syndrome, or unexplained sudden death under 40 years of age in first-degree relatives. · Patients with CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment-induced symptomatic interstitial lung disease (Grade ≥ 2). · Patient with known or family history of severe heart disease, including (a) Experience of any of the following procedures or conditions in the preceding 6 months: coronary artery bypass graft, angioplasty, vascular stent, any other structural heart disease interventions (eg,

- cardiac valve repairmen or replacement surgery or transcatheter valve intervention), severe aortic
