## 5.4.15. Post-marketing experience

Not applicable.

## 5.4.16. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.16.1. Discussion

## 5.4.16.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

Safety data from both SERENA-6 arms (155 patient in both arms), and a camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor combination therapy pool (n= 263), which pools data from SERENA-6 with patients from SERENA-1 treated with camizestrant 75 mg and a CDK4/6 inhibitor, were submitted. Although a monotherapy indication was not sought, additional data from 117 patients treated with camizestrant 75 mg in SERENA-1 was also provided. The 263 patients in the combination therapy pool were regarded as the primary safety pool. The median treatment exposure to camizestrant was 8.3 months in the combination therapy pool, which should be compared to the median PFS in SERENA-6 of 15.97 months. Although the exposure was considered rather short, given that the sought indication is treatment until progression, the safety database is considered sufficient for approval, and the Applicant has committed to provide final safety data post-authorisation, as a recommendation.

The most common adverse events in the combined camizestrant+CDK4/6i pool were hematological (neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased, anemia, leukopenia/white blood cell count decreased), and eye-related (photopsia, dry eyes, visual impairment), but other burdensome AEs were also very common (arthralgia, fatigue, nausea, back pain, diarrhoea, headache, vomiting, asthenia). As expected, most AE rates were comparable between the combination therapy pool and the AI arm of SERENA-6, since both arms received CDK4/6 inhibitors. Especially photopsia was more frequent in the camizestrant arm than the AI arm of SERENA-6 (21.3% vs. 7.7%), and even more frequent in the pooled camizestrant+CDK4/6i population (24%). Similar to all AEs, the most frequent grade ≥3 AEs in the camizestrant+CDK4/6i populations were hematological. Other notable grade ≥ 3 AEs in the combination therapy pool were syncope, QT prolonged, hypertension, vomiting, diarrhoea, abdominal pain, and infections. This has been reflected accordingly in the SmPC section 4.8.

Adverse events of special interest were bradycardia and visual effects. 52 (19.8%) patients in the combination therapy safety pool experienced one or more bradycardia events. Overall, the bradycardic effects of camizestrant appeared well-tolerated in most patients. The maximum reported CTCAE grade was 2 (8 patients). No patients discontinued treatment due to bradycardia. One case of torsades de pointes arrythmia was observed in a patient treated with camizestrant and ribociclib and with concurrent bradycardia and QTc-prolongation. A causal link between camizestrant and the case of torsades de pointes has not been firmly established, but is considered a possibility. Furthermore, 2 syncope events and one death of unkown causes ocurred in the combination therapy pool, which could theoretically be related to arrythmias. The size of the safety pool and the duration of exposure did not appear sufficient to rule out a risk of other rare, life-threatening arrythmias, including bradyarrythmias, associated with conduction disturbances caused by camizestrant, especially (but not only) when combined with ribociclib which is known to cause QT-prolongation. In relation to this concern, it is noteworthy that many patients with cardiac disease or risk factors were excluded from case of torsades des pointes and the above concerns, a warning about the potential arrythmogenic effects of camizestrant in combination with ribociclib has been included in section 4.4 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

SERENA-6 (including atrioventricular block, heart failure, and QT prolongation). Based on the observed The AESI \"visual effects\" was analysed by a grouping of the PTs photopsia, vision blurred, visual impairment, diplopia, photophobia, visual perseveration, and glare. Of these, photopsia was the most common. Most events were mild, with only seven grade 2 and one grade 3 event in the combined safety pool, and no treatment discontinuations or dose reductions due to visual effects. PRO data also indicated that the visual effects were generally well-tolerated. Data from comprehensive ophtalmologic examinations for a small subset of the SERENA-6 patients (approximately 41 patients) were provided, and no structural eye damage was identified. Data on ophtalmological examinations were additionally provided, which seemed to rule out a risk of more serious ophtalmological adverse effects, including retinal damage. Both AESIs have the potential to affect patient's ability to drive and use machines, either due to vision disturbances and possibly dizziness or syncope from bradycardia, and this is included in the SmPC. The number of deaths due to any cause was 22 (8%) in the camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor pool, 20 (12.9%) in the camizestrant arm of SERENA-6 and 18 (11.6%) in the AI arm of SERENA-6 at DC01. Of 22 deaths in the combination therapy pool, 13 were attributed to the disease under investigation by the investigator, and five to \"other\" causes. Possible treatment-related deaths were two patients who died from ADRs which may have beeen linked to interstitial lung disease, a known ADR for CDK4/6 inhibitors, one unexplained death (discussed above), and one death after life-threatening anemia. None of these deaths have been considered related to study treatment.

Serious adverse events included five serious infections, of which two also appeared to have sepsis and neutropenia.

There were only three AEs leading to camizestrant discontinuation in the combination therapy pool. In SERENA-6, nine patients experienced AEs leading to dose reductions, of which four were bradycardia. Importantly, dose reductions will not be possible in the post-approval setting, as only one tablet strength was included in the application.

Patients with moderate and severe renal and hepatic impairment were not included in SERENA-6, and patients with hepatic impairment are expected to have increased exposure to camizestrant due to reduced elimination. Therefore, a warning has been included in section 4.4. of the SmPC that camizestrant should be avoided in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment. There are no apparent patterns observed in the tables on clinical studies in special poulations (see Table 92), probably due to the very small subgroups, so no firm conclusions can be made.

Shifts in laboratory measurements of grade ≥ 3, and measurements at grade 3 or higher were observed for all hematology measurements as well as liver transaminases, which is expected given the known safety profile of the CDK4/6 inhibitors.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There was no potential or confirmed Hy's law cases in the camizestrant arm of SERENA-6, nor in the rest of the combination and monotherapy pools.

Electrocardiogram data showed an increase in mean QTcF by 9.9 msec between baseline and day 15 in the SERENA-6 camizestrant arm, but not the AI arm. This is considered a quite large increase in QTc considering that the threshold for regularory concern is 10 msec (including 95% confidence limits). Some AEs of QT prolonged were observed in the camizestrant safety pools, including one grade 3 event in the monotherapy pool. PK/PD exposure-safety modeling predicted the largest QT-prolonging effects of camizestrant in patients also receiving ribociclib 600 mg. Additionnally a warning about the cardiac safety of the camizestrant + ribociclib combination has been added to the SmPC along with ECG monitoring guidelines. Moreoverthe SmPC was revised to reflect that QT prolonged is considered an ADR to the camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor combination therapy regardless of CDK4/6 inhibitor.

It is considered that overall the SmPC adequately reflects the safety profile of the camizestrant + CDK4/6i as studied.

## 5.4.16.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs presented in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3. above.

## 5.4.16.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Overall, the safety profile of camizestrant in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors appeared acceptable. The majority of the observed serious side effects (including neutropenia and infections) were known from the CDK4/6 inhibitors, with the exception of bradycardia and visual disturbances, which appeared to be specific ADRs for camizestrant. The concern of cardiotoxicity of the camizestrant + CDK4/6i combination has been addressed in the SmPC (warning in 4.4., updated guidance on ECG monitoring in 4.2, list of products to be avoided in 4.5, description of selected AEs in 4.8).

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

Table 94: Summary of safety concerns in the proposed RMP

| Summary of safety concerns Important identified risks   | None   |
|---------------------------------------------------------|--------|
| Important potential risks                               |        |
|                                                         | None   |
| Missing information                                     | None   |

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

Patients with a known or family history of severe structural or ischaemic heart disease were excluded from SERENA-1 and SERENA-6. It remains uncertain whether the bradycardic properties of camizestrant do not confer an additional risk in patients with e.g. structural heart disease or heart failure, as these patients were excluded upfront. Patients with a resting heart rate &lt; 55 bpm, unexplained syncope, symptomatic hypotension, second- and third-degree heart block, or clinically significant sinus pause or sinoatrial block were excluded from the camizestrant clinical studies. The observation that a product with bradycardic properties is safe in patients without pre-existing bradycardia or other bradyarrythmias cannot be used to infer that the product is also safe in patients with pre-existing bradycardia or other bradyarrythmias. Consequently, a warning has been included in the SmPC section 4.4, making clear to the prescriber that the use of camizestrant together with ribociclib can result in cardiotoxicity. In addition, QTc prolongation has been added as a new ADR in the SmPC section 4.8, with a corresponding footnote indicating that it is a known ADR associated with ribociclib. The SmPC section 4.2. has been additionally updated with specific guidance on ECG monitoring, section 4.4 with warnings and precautions on the risk of QT prolongation, and section 4.5 with a list of other products whose concomitant use should be avoided. The case of Torsade de Pointes (TdP) has been mentioned in 4.4, 4.5 and 4.8. Finally, studying a wide spectrum of cardiac comorbidities (\"level of cardiotoxicity\") in the indicated patient population with advanced breast cancer is not considered feasible. Therefore, adequate information in the PI on which patients that have not been studied is deemed sufficient, no further risk characterisation is considered necessary and no addition to the RMP was considered warranted as the warnings included in the SmPC are considered sufficient to mitigate the risk of QTc prolongation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.1. Proposed pharmacovigilance plan.

In the approved RMP version 1.4, routine pharmacovigilance activities were considered sufficient to monitor the safety profile of the product.

No additional pharmacovigilance activities have been proposed.

## 6.2.2. Discussion on the pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.2.1. Routine pharmacovigilance activities

Routine pharmacovigilance has been considered sufficient to identify and characterise the risks of the product.

Routine PhV have been considered as sufficient to monitor the effectiveness of the risk minimisation measures.

## 6.2.2.2. Additional pharmacovigilance activities

There are no planned additional pharmacovigilance activities.

## 8. Product information

## 8.1. Summary of product characteristics (SmPC)

See the attached commented product information.

## 8.1.1. SmPC section 4.1 justification

The approved indication is supported by the results of the pivotal clinical study submitted in the dossier, Serena-6, which evaluated camizestrant + a CDK4/6i versus continuing an AI + a CDK4/6i in the treatment of ER-positive, HER2-negative locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of an ESR1 mutation and without disease progreesion during first-line endocrine-based treatment. The target population in the indication is fully aligned with the population studied in the pivotal trial, including disease and baseline characteristics. The proposed treatment setting is new, as camizestrant is indicated to a biomarker-restricted population based on the emergence of an ESR1 mutation in circulating tumor (ct)DNA during first-line treatment with SoC.

## 8.1.2. SmPC section 4.3 justification

Camizestrant is contraincated when breastfeeding, which is in line with other products of the same class (eg. fulvestrant and imlunestrant).

## 8.3. Additional monitoring

Pursuant to Article 23(1) of Regulation No (EU) 726/2004, Etcamah (camizestrant) is included in the additional monitoring list since it contains a new active substance which, in 1 January 2011, was not contained in any medicinal product authorised in the EU.

Therefore, the summary of product characteristics and the package leaflet include a statement that this medicinal product is subject to additional monitoring and will allow quick identification of new safety information. The statement is preceded by an inverted equilateral black triangle.

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

The final agreed indication is:

\"Etcamah in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with ER-positive, HER2-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation and without disease progression during first-line endocrine therapy in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (for biomarker based patient-selection, see section 4.2 and 5.1).

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, camizestrant plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist or antagonist.\"

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, proposed therapeutic indication

Although many patients are cured of their early-stage disease, advanced breast cancer remains incurable. Approximately 70% of patients present with ER-positive, HER2-negative disease (American Cancer Society 2024-2025), making it the most common subtype in both women and men. Various studies have reported that the frequency of ESR1m is low (approximately 4%) at the initial diagnosis; while approximately 40% of patients at the end of first-line (1L) treatment with an aromatase inhibitor (AI) + a CDK4/6i have detectable ESR1m in ctDNA, and the prevalence of ESR1m rises further to &gt; 50% in patients who have been treated with AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor for more than one year (Bhave et al 2024, Bidard et al 2022, Cardoso et al 2023, Chaudhary et al 2024).

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

For a detailed description, please see section 2.1. of this document.

The preferred standard-of-care first-line therapy for patients with estrogen receptor-positive (ER+), HER2-negative advanced breast cancer is a CDK4/6 inhibitor (CDK4/6i) in combination with endocrine therapy (ET), with improved PFS and OS observed in several phase 3 trials.

There are currently no 1L therapies specifically approved for the treatment of these patients with emergent ESR1m, ahead of disease progression. Progression beyond 1L is often associated with increased toxicities of therapy regimens and diminished treatment efficacy, likely due to the increased genetic complexity of disease and increased tumour burden. Currently, Orserdu (elacestrant) and Inluriyo

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(imlunestrant) are monotherapy options for patients with ESR1m who have progressed on ET + CDK4/6 inhibitor, and modest activity has been shown (median PFS 3.8 months with elacestrant vs 1.9 months for ET's (including AI and fulvestrant) and mPFS 5.5 months with imlunestrant vs 3.8 months for ET's (including AI and fulvestrant). Both Orserdu (elacestrant) and Inluriyo (imlunestrant) are indicated after disease progression under endocrine based regime therapy, placing them as a second line option for patients with an ESR1m detected. According to ESMO Guidelines (Gennari et al 2021, ESMO guideline), evidence based available options for second-line therapy (other than ESR1m targeted options) are fulvestrant-alpelisib (for PIK3CA mutated tumours), PARP inhibitors (for tumours harbouring genomic BRCA mutations), exemestane-everolimus, fulvestrant monotherapy, aromatase inhibitors (AI), trastuzumab deruxtecan (for HER2-low tumours), and chemotherapy. At least two lines of endocrine based therapy are preferred before moving to chemotherapy (Gennari et al, Annals of Oncology, 2021). Thus, there is a need for better treatment options that could be met by earlier identification of patients, who may benefit from therapies that address resistance to current ET in 1L, ahead of clinical and radiological disease progression by targeting the resistance mechanisms that emerge during 1L treatment. This strategy is considered to have the potential to extend the benefit of endocrine based 1L therapy, leading to better patient outcomes for patients with ER-positive, HER2-negative advanced breast cancer.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

The application is primarily based on data from the single pivotal study, SERENA-6, which is a randomised, phase 3, double-blind, placebo-controlled multicentre study. Patients eligible for inclusion were adult female (pre/peri-menopausal or post-menopausal) and male patients with advanced ERpositive, HER2-negative breast cancer and who were treated with an AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor in the firstline advanced setting and who had no evidence of progressive disease. 3325 patients were screened for the presence of an ESR1 mutation using ctDNA, and 315 were randomised 1:1 to continue with standard of care (SoC) or switch the ET backbone for camizestrant, while the CDK4/6i is continued without changes in both arms.

The studied dose of camizestrant was 75mg QD. Study treatment was continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

The primary efficacy endpoint was PFS as assessed by investigator in the ITT. OS and PFS2 were key secondary endpoints.

Of note, the SERENA-6 study investigates a new treatment paradigm, as patients are switched early based on a biomarker-selection (ESR1-mutation) without having a clinical or radiological progression, which is not part of the current standard of care.

## 9.3. Favourable effects

As of the DCO3 corresponding to the third interim analysis of the pivotal study SERENA-6 (02 January 2026), the median duration of follow-up was approximately 23 months.

The primary endpoint of PFS by Investigator was improved by 7.53 months from a median of 9.23 months in the AI arm to 16.76 months in the camizestrant arm. The HR was 0.45 (95%CI: 0.34; 0.59).

At DCO2, the secondary endpoint of PFS by BICR showed a similar HR of 0.46 (95%CI: 0.32, 0.66

At DCO3, the secondary endpoint of PFS2 was met at the time of the planned primary analysis of PFS2 with a HR of 0.63; 95%CI: 0.46, 0.86; p-value 0.00373). Median PFS2 was 25.72 months in the camizestrant arm compared to 19.06 months in the control arm, a difference of 6.66 months.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Immature OS data are available at DCO3, with 30% events observed overall, HR: 0.87 (95%CI: 0.57, 1.30).

Updated subgroup analyses were provided from the DCO2 showing benefit in favour of camizestrant and no discordant results for PFS in the requested subgroups were observed. Hence, the point estimate for HR was still below 1 across important subgroups including the stratification factors: CDK4/6 inhibitor combination partner (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib), ESR1m detectable at first ctDNA test or subsequent ctDNA test, disease site (visceral vs non-visceral), and time from initiation of AI + CDK4/6 inhibitor to randomisation (&lt; 18 months or ≥ 18 months).

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

The collected PFS2 data are considered insufficient to establish a long-term benefit. These data are considered unreliable due the limited number of patients who were continuously followed with tumour assessments according to RECIST 1.1 at appropriate intervals, as well as the heterogeneity in post-study treatment choices.

Although no detrimental effect on OS has been observed at DCO3, the OS data available are immature (30%). Final OS data should be submitted post-authorisation as an Annex II condition to further characterize the efficacy of camizestrant and rule out a detrimental effect on OS.

Finally, the following uncertainties on the biomarker aspects have been considered:

No clinical thresholding for the cut-point defining a tumour as biomarker positive or negative was performed. Due to this, it cannot be excluded that in the threshold definition of biomarker-positivity applied in the pivotal study, patients who may not benefit are included.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

The safety data from both SERENA-6 arms (155 patient in both arms), and a camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor combination therapy pool (n= 263), which pools data from SERENA-6 with patients from SERENA-1 treated with camizestrant 75 mg and a CDK4/6 inhibitor, have been provided. The 263 patients in the combination therapy pool have been regarded as the primary safety pool. The median treatment exposure to camizestrant was 8.3 months in the combination therapy pool.

The most common adverse events in the combined camizestrant+CDK4/6i pool were hematological (neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased, anemia, leukopenia/white blood cell count decreased), and eyerelated (photopsia, dry eyes, visual impairment), but other burdensome AEs were also very common (arthralgia, fatigue, nausea, back pain, diarrhoea, headache, vomiting, asthenia). As expected, most AE rates were comparable between the combination therapy pool and the AI arm of SERENA-6, since both arms received CDK4/6 inhibitors. Of note, photopsia was more frequent in the camizestrant arm than the AI arm of SERENA-6 (20% vs. 7.7%), and even more frequent in the pooled camizestrant+CDK4/6i population (23.2%). Similar to all AEs, the most frequent grade ≥3 AEs in the camizestrant+CDK4/6i populations were hematological. Other notable grade ≥ 3 AEs in the combination therapy pool were syncope, QT prolonged, hypertension, vomiting, diarrhoea, abdominal pain, and infections.

Two ADRs were identified for camizestrant (bradycardia and visual effects), which were also identified as adverse events of special interest. 48 (18.3%) patients in the combination therapy safety pool experienced one or more bradycardia events. Bradycardia is especially concerning given that camizestrant also can prolong the QT interval and may be given together with ribociclib which also prolongs the QT interval. One case of torsades de pointes arrythmia was observed in a patient treated with camizestrant and ribociclib. The AESI \"visual effects\" was analysed by a grouping of the PTs photopsia, vision blurred, visual impairment, diplopia, photophobia, visual perseveration, and glare. Of these, photopsia was the most common.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The number of deaths due to any cause was 22 (8%) in the camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor pool, 20 (12.9%) in the camizestrant arm of SERENA-6 and 18 (11.6%) in the AI arm of SERENA-6. Of 22 deaths in the combination therapy pool, 13 were attributed to the disease under investigation by the investigator, and five to \"other\" causes.

Serious adverse events included five serious infections, of which two also appeared to have sepsis and neutropenia. Other noteworthy SAEs were rare cases of pulmonary embolism, venous thrombosis and unspecified embolisms (one event each), and the above-mentioned case of torsades de pointes.

There were three AEs leading to camizestrant discontinuation in the combination therapy pool. In SERENA-6, nine patients experienced AEs leading to dose reductions, of which four were bradycardia.

Shifts in laboratory measurements of grade ≥ 3, and measurements at grade 3 or higher were observed for all hematology measurements as well as liver transaminases, which is expected given the known safety profile of the CDK4/6 inhibitors.

There was no potential or confirmed Hy's law cases in the camizestrant arm of SERENA-6.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The median treatment exposure to camizestrant was 8.3 months in the combination therapy pool, which is considered rather short, given that the sought indication is treatment until progression, and median PFS in SERENA-6 was 15.97 months. Key limitations of the safety database include its relatively small size in view of the potentially large target population, which limits the detection of rare adverse events, as well as the limited data for the ribociclib and abemaciclib combinations.

The exclusion of patients with significant cardiac comorbidities or severe hepatic impairment upfront further restricts the external validity of the findings.

## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

PFS by INV (primary endpoint) at the DCO3 of 02 January 2026, is mature with 63.1% and 78.5% events in the camizestrant arm vs the control arm, respectively, and a HR of 0.45 (95% CI: 0.34, 0.59). The median PFS by INV was 16.76 months in the camizestrant arm vs 9.23 months in the control arm, resulting in 7.53 months of improvement (at DCO1 it was 6.74 months). The KM curves are clearly separating after 3 months of treatment.

Time to second progression, PFS2, reported a HR 0.63, although the collected data have been considered insufficient to establish long-term benefit. Their reliability is limited by the small number of patients with continuous tumour assessments performed at appropriate intervals according to RECIST 1.1, together with the heterogeneity in post-study treatment choices.

At diagnosis of metastatic breast cancer (MBC), the 5-year survival rate is approximately 38% (Allemani et al., 2018). CDK4/6 inhibitors combined with endocrine therapy (ET) are the standard of care for ERpositive, HER2-negative MBC, demonstrating improved progression-free survival (PFS; median ~2 years), overall survival (OS), and a favourable safety profile (ESMO guidelines).

Following progression on ET plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor, assessment of somatic PIK3CA and ESR1 mutations (optional if further AI is considered), as well as germline BRCA1/2 and PALB2 mutations, is recommended (ESMO guidelines). Second-line ET provides more limited benefit (median PFS 2-4 months for monotherapy; 5-8 months when combined with targeted agents), and the optimal treatment sequence after CDK4/6 inhibitors remains uncertain, depending on prior therapies, duration of response, disease burden, patient preference, and availability (ESMO guidelines).

Early detection of ESR1 mutations, associated with resistance to aromatase inhibitors (AI), may help anticipate treatment failure. In the SERENA-6 study, early switching to camizestrant (plus CDK4/6 inhibitor) based on ESR1 mutation detection resulted in clinically relevant PFS improvement compared with continuing AI until radiological or clinical progression.

However, as this approach involves switching ET prior to disease progression, any PFS benefit should be supported by favourable long-term outcomes and no OS detriment. Moreover, this strategy effectively represents early second-line therapy and entails multiple testings of the blood for ESR1 mutations, which can be challenging for the patients emotionally, since the detection of an ESR1 mutation is associated with treatment-resistance. Only about 10% of the screened population were included in the SERENA-6 trial.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At DCO3, OS is still considered immature with overall 30% events, HR: 0.87 (95% CI: 0.57, 1.30). At this time, there is no apparent detriment on OS, but no clear OS benefit either. In order to further characterise the efficacy of camizestrant, the MAH will submit the final OS analysis as an Annex II.D condition by December 2028.

The benefit of switching to camizestrant is primarily based on the PFS benefit established in the context of a well-conducted randomised, blinded, placebo-controlled study and in the absence of an apparent OS detriment: the efficacy of camizestrant is considered to be sufficiently demonstrated.

Overall, the safety profile of the oral SERD camizestrant in the proposed target population in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors is similar to that known for endocrine therapies. The majority of the observed serious side effects (including neutropenia and infections) are known from the CDK4/6 inhibitors. The main differences observed are bradycardia and visual disturbances, which appear to be specific ADRs for camizestrant. An increased risk of serious arrythmia, including torsade des pointes, remains a concern, and guidelines for ECG monitoring after treatment initiation have been included in the SmPC along with a warning about the concern for cardiotoxicity.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The benefit-risk balance is considered positive for camizestrant + CDK4/6i.

## 9.6.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

## 9.6.3.1. Questions to be posed to additional experts

## 9.6.3.2. Input from additional experts

## 9.6.3.3. Third party intervention(s)

The CHMP received, during the assessment of this application, a correspondence signed by European physicians being specialised in treating breast cancer (hereinafter referred to as \"third party\") expressing the third party views about the SERENA-6 pivotal trial results, about the treatment lines options as well as unmet medical need and supporting the proposed new strategy to target emerging resistance before clinical deterioration. The third party shared in addition a manuscript under press providing a discussion on the rationale, the design, the results and the third party's interpretation of SERENA-6.

The CHMP considered those interventions in the context of its assessment and concluded that the observations put forward by the third party were already known by CHMP, and as such had no impact on the CHMP assessment or its conclusions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

The benefit-risk balance is considered positive for camizestrant + CDK4/6i in the sought indication for adult patients with ER positive, HER2-negative locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation and without disease progression during first-line endcrine therapy in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## APPENDIX

DIVERGENT POSITION DATED 21 May 2026

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## DIVERGENT POSITION DATED 21 May 2026

## Etcamah (camizestrant) EMA/H/C/006494/0000

The undersigned members of the CHMP did not agree with the CHMP's positive opinion recommending the granting of the marketing authorisation in the following indication:

\"Etcamah\" in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) indicated for the treatment of adult patients with ER-positive, HER2-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of ESR1-mutation and without disease progression during first-line endocrine therapy in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (for biomarker based patient-selection, see section 4.2 and 5.1).

In pre- or peri-menopausal women and in men, Etcamah plus a CDK4/6 inhibitor should be combined with a luteinizing hormone releasing hormone (LHRH) agonist or antagonist.\"

The reasons for the divergent opinion are as follows:

The treatment strategy evaluated in the single pivotal SERENA-6 trial does not reflect current standard clinical practice. The study does not adequately answer the clinically relevant question whether early switching endocrine therapy at first detection of an ESR1 mutation, and prior to radiographic or clinical progression, improves long-term patient outcome compared with the established strategy of continuing treatment until progression and switching treatment thereafter.

In particular, this is based on the following issues:

- The SERENA-6 trial primarily evaluates an early-switch treatment strategy triggered by first detection of molecular progression while on first-line treatment. The added clinical value of camizestrant, when introduced at the time when switch of treatment would be indicated as to current standard of care, i.e. at clinical or radiological progression, is not part of the study design.
- Early switching may expose patients with ongoing disease control under the existing regimen to additional treatment-related risks, biomarker-testing burden, and potential loss of time on an effective and tolerated regimen, without robust evidence that the early initiation of camizestrant improves long-term outcome.
- The experimental arm incorporates earlier use of a subsequent endocrine treatment option before conventional first-line treatment failure has been documented.

In consequence, the observed PFS advantage cannot be interpreted in the same manner as in a conventional treatment strategy. It remains to be clarified, whether camizestrant improves the overall treatment sequence or long-term patient outcome compared with the established practice. Therefore, PFS is not considered sufficient as a stand-alone endpoint for pivotal B/R assessment in this context. However, no robust evidence of long-term clinical benefit has been provided to overcome these limitations:

- At present, OS data remain insufficiently mature to be informative (maturity approximately 30%) and do not provide robust evidence that the early-switch strategy improves long-term outcome.
- The submitted PFS2 analysis is considered unreliable due to (i) the heterogeneity in choice and timing of subsequent therapy (partly not reflecting current scientific guideline recommendations), and (ii) the insufficiently standardised assessment of second progression (only a small number of patients was continuously followed with tumour assessments based on RECIST 1.1 methods in appropriate time intervals).

Therefore, based on the currently available SERENA-6 data, we cannot conclude on a positive benefit-risk balance for Etcamah in the proposed indication.

Janet König

Jan Müller-Berghaus Paolo Gasparini Eva Skovlund Carolina Prieto Fernandez Sol Ruiz Peter Mol Hrefna Gudmundsdottir Christian B. Roes
